Escape Backpack / Norse / Brainstorm v2

The Raven Inheritance

Your great-aunt has sent you her field bag and the first postcard from a long journey. More cards are hidden inside. Together they lead to the invitation she wants you to earn.

Working titlePlayful mysteryAdults + teensReusable propsA notch harder than HikingDesigner copy · spoilers
Current decisions

Approved changes · 22 September 2026

Complete adopted mechanisms, postcard copy drafts and unfinished production work.

Approved by the user · 22 September 2026. G7 uses coin values and an upside-down tally. G8 keeps HRAFN and adds the five-perch conversion to a four-digit lock. These replace weighing and the cryptex. The remaining review recommendations retain their own proposal status.
RV-20 · Approved mechanism

The treasure tally

Sort three hoards by currency → convert the Viking pile's coins → add to 3705 → rotate → SOLE.

Superseded in part, 24 Sept 2026: the three currencies are released loose and mixed in one pouch, not as separate piles; the cache mark is the fehu rune beside the treasure cave, matched to the ticket’s Coin Room key. See the hoard prop.
Decided, 23 September 2026: the hoard is three separate piles, one per real-history currency, not one mixed hoard sorted by a single selection mark. Each pile is its own small "natural find" — a few common coins, plus one rare standout. The cache mark tells players which pile (currency) matters; a merchant's key gives each pile's coins a conversion rate to a common value. Only the Viking pile's converted total is the puzzle's answer — the other two piles are decoys with no required total.

The three piles

  • Islamic dirhams — 5 coins (decoy). The most common foreign silver actually found in Viking hoards. No target sum.
  • Carolingian deniers — 4 coins (decoy). Frankish trade silver, rarer than dirhams. No target sum.
  • Viking/Norse coins — 3 coins (the target). One of each rarity tier: common Hedeby coin (rate 5), mid York imitation penny (rate 100), rare Anlaf Guthfrithsson "raven" penny (rate 3600). 5 + 100 + 3600 = 3705. The raven penny is a deliberate callback to the HRAFN/raven thread elsewhere in the game.
  1. The cache mark selects the Viking pile from the three sorted hoards.
  2. The merchant's key gives each of that pile's three coins its conversion rate. Add all three: 3705.
  3. Record the total on the supplied calculator or four-wheel tally, labelled “Use this to tally treasure.”
  4. A3 tells players to turn the whole display upside down. Calculator-style 5, 0, 7, 3 read left-to-right as S, O, L, E after rotation.
  5. SOLE releases decoy card AD and the comb (step 8 of PC-18). Superseded, 25 Sept 2026: this step read “SOLE opens G7 and releases the initial Harald materials, including the perch card.” Those now come from BOOK.

No grams, weighing, decimal point, rounding or discarded digit. Preserve the physical sorting and word-reveal moment.

Two supported tally choices

  • Calculator: lowest-build prototype. Check the actual shapes of 3, 7 and 5 and whether 3705 remains displayed when handled. Label its job; test reset/power.
  • 3D-printed tally: four independently settable 0–9 wheels with one clear reading window. Players do the addition and set the result manually; it is not an adding machine. Use suitable segmented numerals and stable wheel positions so rotating the whole prop does not alter the digits. Reset to 0000.

Decided 25 Sept 2026: the 3D-printed wheel tally (PR-24; files in Props/SoleTally/, not yet print-tested). Still open: physical coin design/fabrication and the printed merchant's key (PR-25). Coin counts, currencies, rates and the target total are decided; no coin set or physical display is claimed tested.

RV-21 · Approved mechanism

The raven’s flights

Decode HRAFN → visit H–R–A–F–N → read 2–6–4–8.

Keep H5’s underlined RAVEN selector, H4’s branch-rune key and the corrected Harald map. The five decoded letters identify five visits among eight possible perches: H, R, T, O, A, N, F, S.

  1. Start at H, then follow the direct drawn connection to R.
  2. Continue directly R→A, A→F and F→N, using the decoded letter order.
  3. Read one number per connection, in flight order: 2648. Do not add numbers, count line lengths, translate HRAFN or find a shortest route.
  4. Use one four-digit lock directly on the board-kit pouch. It contains H1, the ticket, board, pieces and challenge rules.

Player-facing card

Working size: one half-Letter sheet, 8.5×5.5 inches, subject to print-size proof. Title: “The raven’s flights.” Clue: “Follow the five letters you uncovered. Read the number on each flight, in order.” Four blank answer boxes. All connections look alike; directions come from the letters. No route highlight, HRAFN or answer on the player copy.

Release: inside G7 with H4/H5 and Harald’s map, outside the G8 board pouch. Match the perch card and pouch with a small raven emblem so the four digits have an obvious destination. The card is an extra prop, not another postcard.

The code and pictured network are adopted. Final printable artwork and its physical test remain open (PR-26). Existing cryptex, key and keyed-closure proposals are superseded. The number of puzzle gates stays eleven in the review flow.

Exact perch connections — production reference

Every connection is two-way and carries one digit. The network has no line crossings. Only the decoded letter order selects the route.

Replaces “Whatever pile the map marks for you, weigh it before anything else. Don’t guess the number — read it.”

Superseded, 25 Sept 2026: not used. The user rewrote the whole A3 message; see the built text in the Postcards tab.

Optional playtest cue · Counting the treasure

Match the cache mark to the coins. Use the merchant’s key to add the value of every matching coin. Record the whole-number total on the treasure tally. Keep your tally beside Liv’s postcards.

Keep the flip instruction on A3. This cue supports the prototype if the draft A1 has not yet been printed; it must replace the old balance cue.

Check before printing or packing

  • Independently total the selected coin set to 3705; check selection and value marks are distinguishable. Coins are fictional game currency, not a historical-value claim.
  • Show the actual rotated tally to someone unfamiliar with SOLE. Check wheel stability or calculator display, and that the word lock supports S–O–L–E.
  • From the corrected rune map and current H4/H5, decode HRAFN and follow only direct flights to get 2648. Check readability at final card size without an answer key.
  • Verify that the perch card is accessible before the board lock and that G8 directly secures the complete board kit. Keep H1 inside G8 and H6 inside G10.
  • Rebuild both A1/A3 print formats and their gallery back images together when the copy goes to print. Recheck preserved finale clues and keep the deck count unchanged.
Superseded mechanism history

G7: weighing to 370.56 g, truncating to 370.5 and deleting the decimal point is retired. No scale or calibration is required by the approved tally version.

G8: HRAFN as the setting on a five-letter cryptex, followed by a key to a second board-kit closure, is retired. HRAFN now selects four flight digits and G8 is one direct four-digit lock.

Original puzzle snapshots and old PDFs remain available as historical source material. They do not override these approved replacements.

Open the updated review checklist and packing plan

Approved update, 22 September 2026: coin values total 3705 and rotate to SOLE; HRAFN selects four flight digits, 2648, for a lock directly on the board pouch. The scale and cryptex are superseded. Full specifications, visual and postcard changes. Older options below retain their historical status.
Working direction

A family secret, carried by postcards

Your great-aunt Liv has been tracing the family’s Nordic origins. Her first postcard arrives with her locked field bag. Solving what she left behind releases more postcards from more places.

The postcards are the game. Everything else in the bag exists to act on them: the tally turns a coin total into a word, the perches turn decoded letters into four digits, and the board is set up by a postcard. Players use the cards as ordinary clues along the way, and at the end the whole deck becomes one final answer.

Keep the original ending

The final route code opens the last container. Inside is the plane ticket from your idea deck, plus: “I could use someone who notices what everyone else walks past.”

The ticket is a fictional game prop. The candidate routes form 1972: Leif 1, Rollo’s family line 9, Aud 7 and Harald 2. Her travel year is now dated and verified — 2 Feb to 15 Dec, variable stay lengths, in the Final riddle tab. The printed maps are now settled (PZ-06): four laminated letter sheets, drawn on with wet-erase marker. The dig is in Norway, but the site itself and what it is for are still open (ST-05). Decoy postcards and the family crest/logo filter are now part of the candidate ending too — see the Final riddle tab.

Why it is locked at all

Liv loves setting family challenges. This parcel is her invitation test. Each stage protects a piece of her research and releases the journey a few places at a time.

The recurring “aha”

Every card does a job once, so players keep it. At the end every card does a second job, and the deck they have been carrying turns out to be the map of her whole trip.

This sequence is deliberately incomplete. Two anchors are settled — the first puzzle and the last. Everything between them is open, and so is the number and naming of the containers the bag is divided into. That is on purpose: the middle should be designed out of the postcard system rather than fitted around a pocket list decided in advance. Candidates live in the Options tab; everything unresolved is tracked in Open questions.
Settled

What we know

Two anchors

First puzzle · a four-digit code

The bag arrives locked, with the first postcard and two luggage tags attached to it. Each tag has a printed “If found, please return to” line that Liv never filled in properly — instead she hand-wrote the address of a hotel she stayed at, and the street number is the only number on the tag.

Postcard L1 is from L’Anse aux Meadows and names the place. The tag addressed to a L’Anse aux Meadows hotel therefore carries the first two digits; the other tag carries the last two.

Code 1021 — the year tree-ring dating placed the Norse at L’Anse aux Meadows. Tag A: Vinland Trail Lodge, 10 Skipper’s Wharf, L’Anse aux Meadows, NL. Tag B: Hôtel Rollon, 21 avenue Rollo, Rouen, France. Note what the postcard is doing: it supplies the ordering rule, not the data. That is the standard the rest of the game should hold to.

Last puzzle · four maps and 22 postcards

Players finish with four printed maps and 22 postcards — the 18 real stops plus one decoy per trail. A note in the final bundle (the family logo and crest) lets players filter out the four decoys before tracing. Placing each trail’s real cards and drawing its legs in the right order traces a shape, and the four shapes are the four digits of the final code.

Candidate code 1972: Leif 1, Rollo 9, Aud 7, Harald 2. The shapes still have to be tested at true print size — the family 9 is the riskiest. Whether the cards are pinned, connected with cord, or read under an overlay is undecided. The 22-card, decoy-filtered version is a candidate direction, not yet proofed — see the Final riddle tab.

Click a step for its full mechanism

Every step, with its status

This grid is generated from the puzzle data, not typed by hand. It shows whatever is currently recorded in Puzzles & locks, in play order, so it cannot drift out of date the way a written list does — add or change a puzzle there and this sequence follows. It is an order, not a fixed one: container structure is still unfrozen (ST-01), so several steps end in a code with nothing yet assigned to open.

What this borrows from Hiking

Keep

Locked pockets, tactile props, clues saved for later, a final answer built from collected pieces and a warm personal reward.

Give Norse its own identity

Weathered field notes, a miniature board, museum-style object labels, and a deck of postcards that is the spine rather than the dressing. Keep rune translation to one short puzzle so the rest feels varied.

Endgame candidate · decoy filter + evidence-led calendar

The final riddle

22 postcards are in hand: the 18 real stops plus one decoy per trail. The final bundle also holds a note showing two drawings — the family logo (3 elements) and the family crest (6 elements) — that lets players tell real stops from decoys. Players then rebuild the order of Liv’s interleaved travel year from what she writes on the cards, backed by four dated museum tickets and a small final bundle of cross-trail evidence, trace each real trail on its map, and read the digits in order of each trail’s first appearance.

Status: mechanism and itinerary verified, 17 Sept 2026. The solver check called for in “Validation” has been written and run — tests 1, 2 and 4 pass, test 3 is geometric and still open. The deck, the filter, the itinerary, the dates and the clue set are decided. What remains open is the per-card element mark, its physical form, and the rest of the bundle’s contents (PZ-18). Five already-built cards need one added clause each before printing — listed in the clue set below.
All four legs settled, 20 Sept 2026. The evidence model is in section 2c and the four legs in 2d–2g. Tools/final_riddle_clues.py and the solving board in section 6 both encode it and agree. What is left is production, not design: the props do not exist yet, and the generated visuals in 2i still describe the old interleaved year. (24 Sept 2026: the five Rollo-card sentences and the one on H3 are confirmed deleted from both the build scripts and the printed PDFs.)
Decided, 19 Sept 2026 — each leg runs contiguously in time, and dated props carry the order. Liv no longer interleaves the four trails across the year. She completes Leif’s leg, then Rollo’s, then Aud’s, then Harald’s. Three things follow, and together they replace most of the postcard-text clue set.

1 — The four museum tickets now carry the leg order outright. (Corrected 20 Sept 2026 — they do not. Players collect them during play, so this broke the ambiguity requirement. Three transition tickets carry it instead, inside the bundle. See section 2c below.) One dated ticket per leg, and because the legs no longer overlap, sorting those four tickets by date is the order the digits read. This closes the delivery gap found earlier today: PZ-08 decided the digits read in first-appearance order, but nothing in the game ever told a team that, so four digits and four maps arrived with no stated reason to arrange them Leif–Rollo–Aud–Harald. Four tickets in date order now say it without a rule having to be written down anywhere.

2 — The final bundle becomes transition tickets. (Still the model, 20 Sept 2026 — briefly replaced by four return itineraries and then restored, once it was settled that Liv winters abroad rather than flying home. See 2c.) Each is a travel document from the end of one leg to the start of the next — for example a flight out of Leif’s last stop into Rollo’s first. One object therefore proves two facts: that card closes its leg, and that card opens the next one. Three transitions cover all four legs. This supersedes the earlier bundle of assorted boarding passes and receipts.

3 — Within a leg, month references in her handwriting do the ordering. A card that mentions a November day, against a leg whose museum ticket is dated October, sits after the museum stop. The month names its own anchor, so it survives any card layout — which the retired “XXX next” phrasing did not.

Why the old clues had to go. “Winchester next” and “Kyiv next” were deictic: they point at a card that is not the one you are holding. Collect the deck in a different order, or lay it out differently, and the referent is gone. Worse, that phrasing was never intended as a mechanism — it is simply how the user writes — and the solver had quietly promoted incidental voice into load-bearing evidence. The rule from here: an ordering clue must name its own anchor. A dated prop is strongest, card text naming the other place or event is fine, and a bare “next” or “then” with no named referent is not a clue at all. Her voice keeps the phrasing; the clue set stops depending on it.
Leg 1 settled, 19 Sept 2026 — Leif moves to late August / mid-September. February was chosen so L3’s aurora would work, and it quietly broke the rest of the leg. The itinerary table already conceded that L’Anse aux Meadows’ visitor centre is shut in February and that Battle Harbour is not credible in winter. That compromise is no longer survivable, because a closed visitor centre cannot issue the dated admission ticket the leg order now depends on. A third problem was unrecorded: LD Brattahlíð’s “green against the ice” is false in February — the Eastern Settlement greens up roughly June to September.

One window satisfies all four cards. L’Anse aux Meadows and Battle Harbour are both inside their visitor seasons; Brattahlíð is still green; and at 67.6°N Qikiqtarjuaq’s midnight sun has ended and real darkness has returned, so the aurora on L3 works and “the cold” stays credible.

Newfoundland → Labrador → Greenland → Baffin is a clean south-to-north run, and the leg straddles a month boundary, which is what the month-reference device needs.

Leif now needs no ordering text beyond L1’s opening line. L1 states it is first. The Leif→Rollo ticket, out of Qikiqtarjuaq, proves L3 closes it. L2 is second by elimination, and LD is removed by the crest. Accordingly L2’s “Second stop: Markland” was rewritten to “Markland today” and rebuilt, matching L3’s earlier change — neither card numbers itself any more, and neither needs to.

Note for the rewrite pass: L2’s Fun Fact calls Markland “the second landfall on the Vinland voyages.” That is a fact about the sagas, not about Liv’s trip, and it is being kept — but it should be read once more at proof stage in case it now looks like a position hint.

Resolved, 20 Sept 2026 — every prop this model needs now exists. This block recorded three gaps when the legs were first blocked. All three are closed: the journal page is built (Props/Journal), the three transition tickets are built (Props/Tickets), and the museum tickets no longer need dates at all — leg order moved to the transition chain, so the dating question that looked urgent here simply went away. One or two may still be left deliberately undated for texture, but nothing depends on it.
1 · The deck and the filter

22 cards, four of them decoys

One decoy per trail

Each trail gets one decoy postcard: a place Liv really visited that is not part of that storyline. Decoys do earlier puzzle jobs like every other card, so nothing marks them as unused ahead of time. Decided, 15 Sept 2026: that job has to be a real lock, the same standard every other card meets, not just flavour or a calendar statement — see the Postcard system tab. Brattahlíð already meets it (the beasts-chain imprint); Walcheren now does too, combined with Châlus and Roumare in the "counting in the illustration" lock (PZ-19); Bjarnarhöfn now does too, paired with the comb to gate entry to Harald's leg (PZ-17); Constantinople is now assigned too — it carries one third of the MEAD riddle (PZ-20), a rebus for FERMENTED, alongside H2 and H3.

Which drawing covers which trail

The 3-element symbol covers the two 3-stop trails (Leif, Aud); the 6-element crest covers the two 6-stop trails (Rollo, Harald). Each real trail contains every element of its drawing exactly once, so players can confirm the filter before ordering anything.

Decided, 15 Sept 2026 — elements: symbol = raven, longship, compass rose/sun-wheel. Crest = battle-axe, shield, wolf, anchor, drinking horn, valknut. Which specific element sits on which real stop’s card is still open.

Visual form revised, 19 Sept 2026: the 3-element symbol is a circular knotwork emblem divided into three equal compartments, giving the raven, longship and sun-wheel comparable size and weight. This supersedes the raven-dominant first version. The 6-element crest remains a family coat of arms — a Norse-styled heraldic shield with six fields, one charge each. Full generation briefs for both: PR-22.

Fake elements can’t be counted out

The Leif and Aud decoys share one fake element; the Rollo and Harald decoys share another. Every element in the deck appears exactly twice, and only the drawings expose which two are fake. Fakes must look like they belong on a crest, so they can’t be dismissed on style alone.

Decided, 15 Sept 2026 — fakes: Leif/Aud decoys share Mjölnir (Thor’s hammer). Rollo/Harald decoys share a crown — chosen because both trails are about rulers, which is exactly what makes it convincing.

Revised, 19 Sept 2026 — artwork built, subjects changed: Leif/Aud decoys now share a horned helmet; Rollo/Harald decoys now share a double-sided axe. Both are stronger fakes than what they replace, for the same reason — each is the iconic "wrong" image for its own theme rather than a generic Norse-adjacent object. The horned helmet is the single most famous Viking myth (real Norse helmets had no horns). The double-sided axe deliberately reuses HI-01's own historical correction — a labrys is Minoan, not Norse, and Harald's Varangian Guard actually carried a single-bladed Dane axe — so the fake is the exact mistake the design guide already caught once, now working as the in-fiction trap instead of a corrected error. Hand-drawn postcard marks for both are built: Art/FinalPuzzle/PostcardMarks/Decoy_Horned_Helmet_HandDrawn_v1.png and Decoy_Double_Axe_HandDrawn_v1.png. The double axe is a two-bladed symmetrical head on a short haft, visually distinct from the real single-bladed battle-axe crest element (one asymmetric blade, longer haft) — checked side by side at a simulated 0.4 in mark size and the two silhouettes read apart cleanly, unlike the shield/sun-wheel pair above.

The element mark itself

Visually separate from the stamp (trail) and the postmark (place) — for example a wax-seal impression or a small mark in the picture border. Deliberately not UV: the blacklight prop arrives early, and players would scan every card before the rule means anything. Needs a legibility test at card size under table lighting.

Decoys still have to earn their place on the map

Each decoy city must sit on its trail’s map so that including it draws a plausible wrong digit — that’s what stops a team from eyeballing the 1 and the 7 straight off the map.

Decided, 15 Sept 2026 — decoy cities: Leif = Brattahlíð / Qassiarsuk, Greenland. Rollo = Walcheren, Netherlands. Aud = Bjarnarhöfn, Iceland. Harald = Constantinople/Istanbul. All four are real Liv-visited places already in the research corpus (stops.js), just not on that trail’s six postcard stops. None is shape-checked against its map projection yet — that test, and the four decoy cards’ text, are still open.

2 · The itinerary

Liv’s travel years

Aug 2024 – Jul 2025 · one leg at a time

Re-dated 20 Sept 2026 for the blocked-leg model, and regenerated from Tools/build_final_riddle_visuals.py so the table cannot drift from the drawings below it. Liv completes each leg before starting the next and the journey spans two years: Leif and Rollo in the autumn of 2024, then a winter abroad, then Aud and Harald in May–July 2025. 79 nights away, 24 Aug 2024 to 26 Jul 2025. The last column is a credibility check, not a clue — under rule 3 no team may be asked to infer anything from a season, but a card describing a place at an impossible time of year still breaks the fiction. Aud’s and Harald’s exact days inside their windows are a first pass and can move freely; nothing in the clue set depends on them.

Why the slot grid had to go, and what replaced it. Under a uniform 3-day slot a date was a position — (date − start) ÷ 3 + 1 — so a single dated ticket excluded every other card from that slot, and that arithmetic carried most of the deduction. Variable stays destroy it: a date now tells you only the order of the dated props among themselves. The previous clue set was re-run under the weaker semantics and collapsed to 18+ answers across more than 400,000 valid calendars. The set below is rebuilt to be purely ordinal — the order is carried by what Liv writes, not by arithmetic on dates. That is precisely what frees the stay lengths to vary. Two rules are retired with the grid: “every stated gap is a multiple of 3 days” (no clue states a gap in days any more) and the slot-exclusion deduction.

First appearances land at positions 1, 4, 6 and 8 — Leif, Rollo, Aud, Harald — so the digits still read 1 9 7 2 under PZ-08’s first-appearance rule.

Four already-built cards became correct by moving to a Feb–Dec year. H1 Oslo’s “a whole week in Oslo” is now a true seven nights rather than a contradiction of the slot length; LD Brattahlíð’s “green against the ice” moved from March, when Greenland is white, to June; L3’s aurora sits in February; H5’s gelato sits in August. One card had to change instead of being rescued: L2 Battle Harbour, whose “something with claws walked past my tent” is not credible on a boat-access island in February. Changed to “past the cabin” and rebuilt, 17 Sept 2026, keeping the joke. Positions 1–2 are locked to Leif by printed card text (L3’s own anchor was removed on 19 Sept 2026, see below), so the journey still starts when Leif starts and the leg could not simply be moved to summer. L3’s February aurora is now justified by the itinerary alone rather than by printed text — no in-game contradiction, since the card carries no date.
2b · The clue set

Every clue, and what it fixes

Superseded 20 Sept 2026
Superseded, 20 Sept 2026 — kept for the record only. The table below is the 17 Sept clue set. Most of its rows break the evidence rules adopted on 20 Sept: cards that state their own position, and clues that point at a card other than the one in hand. The current spec is section 2c. Read this table as history — it is still useful for seeing which sentences are physically printed on built cards, and therefore what has to be deleted.

Verified as a set (see Validation below). Do not prune the rows without re-running the solver: clues that test as individually redundant are not jointly redundant — removing six “redundant” clues together broke uniqueness from 1 answer to 8 during this pass.

Applied, 17 Sept 2026. All five added clauses are now in their build scripts and both PDFs plus the gallery PNG are rebuilt for each. The exact wording as printed is in PZ-05. The solver was re-run afterwards and the answer is still unique.

Two of the five needed care. R2 Rouen is the most crowded card in the deck — about one spare line — and it also carries the word-lock reading order (PZ-15), so the new sentence was placed after all six Saxon words and deliberately contains none of them; the order fight → forest → cow → people → poultry → inn is unchanged. H4 Hedeby draws the branch-rune key table below the signature, and that box clamps at y = 30, so one extra line of message pushed its caption out of the frame. The paragraph was tightened by a few words to stay at ten lines rather than moving the table. All 18 real cards are now written and built (19 Sept 2026, H6 last).

The element-mark re-print is still ahead: every one of the 22 cards needs one, so that pass is where the crest/logo marks should land.
Decided and applied, 19 Sept 2026 — L3 no longer numbers itself. Card L3 Qikiqtarjuaq opened “Third stop: Helluland…”; it now opens “New stop: Helluland…”. The rest of the card is unchanged, and the C('pos', ('L3', 3)) row is gone from final_riddle_clues.py.

The logic is untouched. L1 and L2 still pin positions 1 and 2 by their own printed text, and L3 is Leif’s only other real card, so L3 third is forced by elimination. Re-run confirms the same single answer with the bundle and the same 3 answers without it. A direct probe found zero calendars in which L3 precedes L2, so this is a real result and not an artefact of the enumeration cap.

What it buys is in the room, not in the solver. Previously three of the four Leif-stamped cards numbered themselves, which exposed LD Brattahlíð as “the one with no number” from the card text alone — a team could skip the crest filter on Leif entirely. Now two Leif cards carry no position statement, so telling the third real stop from the decoy requires the crest. It also partly answers the decoy complaint in the panel note above: the unfiltered trace is no longer a single option. L1 → L2 → L3 → LD still only adds a hook, but L1 → L2 → LD → L3 runs east to Greenland and back west to Baffin, which reads as a V rather than a 1.

One cost, recorded rather than solved. The digit itself gains nothing — a 1 has no internal shape, so either endpoint still draws a plausible stroke. This buys “the crest is needed”, not “the shape self-checks”. Open under PZ-18.

L3 keeps both of its jobs — corrected 19 Sept 2026. An earlier draft of this note claimed the position statement was L3’s only contribution and that the card now fell short of the 15 Sept “every card’s job 3 gates a lock” rule. That was wrong on both counts. L3 is still one of the three map points that draw Leif’s 1, and it is still placed third — by elimination rather than by its own text. It also still gates a physical lock: under PZ-13 the L2/L3 hold-to-light join opens lock 3, which has nothing to do with the opening sentence. And an ordering statement was never what satisfied job 3 in the first place — that rule explicitly excludes “a calendar statement” on its own. So what was removed here is a clue, not a job: L3 is now a consumer of the ordering deduction instead of a source of it, and nothing structural was lost.
Do not also remove L2’s “Second stop”. Tested on 19 Sept 2026: with both L2’s and L3’s anchors gone, L3 before L2 becomes reachable and Leif’s order is no longer forced. The solver still reports one answer in that configuration, but that is a false pass — the reversed calendars sit beyond the 400,000 enumeration cap. A direct probe finds them immediately. Any future clue cut on this leg must be checked with a probe, not with the answer count alone.
2c · The evidence model

What counts as a clue, and where it is allowed to live

Current spec · 20 Sept 2026

This supersedes the 17 Sept clue set in 2b. It came out of a working session that started from one objection — “Winchester next” is meaningless if the deck is collected or laid out differently — and ended up replacing most of how the endgame carries its evidence.

The five rules.

1 — No card states its own position. No “first stop”, no “second stop”, no “the last of Rollo’s places”. A card that numbers itself is doing a prop’s job badly.
2 — No deictic clues. Nothing that points at a card other than the one in hand: no “next”, no “then”, no “after that” with nothing named. Collect the deck in another order and the referent is gone.
3 — Every clue names its anchor — a place, another prop, or something the map actually shows. No outside knowledge: not the weather, not a museum’s opening season, not which port serves which town, not which town has a tapestry unless a card in the deck says so.
4 — Decoys read as genuine stops. No “detour”, no “before really starting”. A decoy that flags itself is not a decoy. This reverses an earlier decision made the same week.
5 — Ambiguity holds until the final bundle. Both the decoys and the leg order must be unresolvable before the reveal.

Her voice is not the target. The user writes “Kyiv next” because that is how they talk, and the phrasing can stay on the card. What changes is that the clue set stops treating incidental voice as evidence — which is what the solver had quietly been doing.

Layer 1 · during play

Postcards, the museum tickets as they are won, and the four printed maps. Postcards carry only natural cross-references — the model is card R5 Battle’s “especially after spending hours looking at the tapestry,” which is the sentence you would write anyway and happens to place it after Bayeux. Not every museum ticket carries a date; one or two are deliberately undated so the tickets cannot be sorted into a leg order early. Which ones stay dated is open

Result: partial ordering inside some legs. No leg order. No way to tell a decoy from a real stop.

Layer 2 · the final bundle

Two kinds of object, and nothing else. The journal page carries the filter and most of the remaining ordering. Three transition tickets chain the four legs end-to-start, and their dates give the leg order. Both are withheld until the reveal, which is what makes rule 5 hold.

Result: the decoys fall out, the leg order locks, and the last ordering gaps close.

The bundle, part one

Three transition tickets, and two cards that name their own end

Each ticket is a travel document from the end of one leg to the start of the next, so one object proves two things at once. Three tickets chain all four legs, and their dates give the leg order.

TicketClosesOpens
Leif → RolloL3 QikiqtarjuaqRD Walcheren
Rollo → AudR6 Roumare ForestA1 Dögurðarnes
Aud → HaraldA3 EsjubergH1 Oslo
The chain has two loose ends, and the cards cover them. No ticket precedes Leif and none follows Harald, so the journey’s first and last stops are stated on the cards themselves: L1 “the first stop on my journey” and H6 “Patara is the last stop of my travels for now.” These are the only two cards in the deck permitted to state their own position, and they are exactly the two ends of the whole journey — a principled exception to rule 1 rather than a leak. Every other card is placed by a ticket, the filter, a journal entry or elimination.
Superseded, 20 Sept 2026 — an intermediate design used four return itineraries instead. The argument was that a single ticket cannot span the winter between Rollo and Aud once the journey runs across two years. It can: Liv simply stays abroad over the winter rather than flying home, so the gap is a long stay, not a break in the chain. Three tickets, not eight flights.

A ticket may land on a decoy — the Leif→Rollo one lands on Walcheren. That is fine: it fixes where the block starts, not which real card is first, and it keeps the decoy embedded in the journey as rule 4 wants. Where it happens the leg needs one extra piece of evidence for its real opener, which is why Rollo carries a Walcheren–Châlus journal entry while Leif, Aud and Harald carry none.

Built, 20 Sept 2026 — NorseBackpack/Props/Tickets/build_transition_tickets_pdf.py. Three printed booking confirmations, 5.4 × 2.5 in each. Outputs: Transition_Tickets_Print.pdf (one per page) and Letter_Print.pdf (all three on one sheet with trim guides). Machine-set in Helvetica on near-white stock — these are travel-agency paperwork, not her hand, and they should look nothing like the postcards or the journal page.
TicketClosesOpensSegments
Leif → RolloL3 QikiqtarjuaqRD Walcheren4 — via Iqaluit, Ottawa, Amsterdam
Rollo → AudR6 Roumare ForestA1 Dögurðarnes4 — via Rouen, Paris, Reykjavík
Aud → HaraldA3 EsjubergH1 Oslo2 — via Reykjavík
Why these are multi-segment itineraries and not boarding passes. The endpoints the puzzle needs are not airports. Roumare is a forest; Dögurðarnes and Esjuberg are Icelandic farmsteads; and there is no direct Qikiqtarjuaq–Netherlands service. A boarding pass could only name airports, and “which airport serves which town” is precisely the outside knowledge rule 3 forbids — the same trap that made us reject naming Ouistreham for the Channel crossing. A booking confirmation lists every waypoint, so the two place names that carry the clue are printed in full, and the first origin and last destination are set in bold to make them the ones the eye lands on.
What is load-bearing on these props, and what is not. Only the first origin and the last destination of each ticket. Those two place names are the whole clue. Carriers, service numbers, booking references, e-ticket numbers and the intermediate hops are all dressing, invented for the look, and can be changed freely without touching the puzzle. The routings are plausible rather than verified — real services exist on these city pairs, but no timetable has been checked, and none needs to be.

Place names are printed without diacritics — “Dogurdarnes”, “Reykjavik” — which is how airline systems really do it, and reads as authentic rather than sloppy. The postcards keep the correct spelling, and the match is still obvious.
One invention that needs a decision: Liv’s surname. The tickets need a passenger name and no surname for her is recorded anywhere in the project. The cards are addressed to her nephew John Ericson, so she is printed as ERICSON / LIV, which also quietly supports the shared-family-crest premise. It is one constant at the top of the build script — change it there if a different family name is ever settled. Nothing else depends on it.
The bundle, part two

The journal page: “Family iconography”

One sheet, two halves. It is the keystone of the whole endgame.

Top · the crest and the symbol

The two drawings and their nine elements, unannotated. This is the filter: a card carrying none of the nine is not one of her stops.

Bottom · travel highlights

A list of journeys, each with a memorable detail and no reference to the elements at all. For example: “Train, Rouen – Bayeux — a carriage full of French students, all loudly excited about seeing the tapestry.”

Why the two halves must not refer to each other. An earlier draft had the notes explain where each element came from — “I thought of the horn on the train from Rouen to Bayeux.” That reads as though the horn belongs to one of those two stops, which corrupts the filter. Decoupling them fixes it, and buys something better: the highlights are unusable until the filter above them has been applied, because some entries name decoy stops. A team reading the list cold reconstructs a route through places that are not hers. The page teaches its own use, top to bottom.
Four entries in total, across the whole journey. Rollo needs three — Walcheren–Châlus, Rouen–Bayeux, Bayeux–Winchester — and Harald one, Staraya Ladoga–Kyiv. Leif and Aud need none: their four-card legs fall out of the tickets and the filter alone. The page is therefore lopsided, with three entries on one leg and one on another, which may read oddly for a travel document. Texture entries can even that out, but every one must be checked against the enumerator first — naming two stops in a single entry constrains them whether or not that was the intent.
Group the entries by transport, not by date — trains, ferries, flights. A real highlights page clusters that way, and it stops the list from giving away the chronology through its own ordering. It also lets decoy stops appear as ordinary texture rather than as something obviously planted.
A journey entry means consecutive, not merely earlier. “Train, Rouen – Bayeux” says she went directly, so nothing sat between them. This is much stronger than an ordering relation and it is easy to under-count: working Rollo’s leg on the weaker reading suggested four entries were needed and that a Walcheren entry was wasted. On the correct reading three entries close the leg and the Walcheren entry is one of them.
Built, 20 Sept 2026 — NorseBackpack/Props/Journal/build_journal_page_pdf.py. A5 portrait, so it prints two-up on Letter; a Letter version with a trim guide is written alongside. Outputs: Journal_Family_Iconography_Print.pdf and Letter_Print.pdf.
Set in her handwriting throughout on the deck’s own paper colour — it is her notebook, not a printed form. The top half uses PR-22’s two reference drawings as they stand; the crest’s blank banner is left blank.

The drawings are deliberately large. A team’s actual task is comparing a 23 pt mark on a postcard against these, so size is a usability gain rather than decoration. The first layout left roughly a third of the page empty below the entries; the art was enlarged and the sections spread until the page filled.
The element names are listed under each drawing, and two of them had to be sharpened. Naming the nine is safe — naming which card carries which would not be — and the names matter because the card marks are loose hand-drawn doodles while the reference drawings are detailed engravings. A name bridges that gap.

But “axe” alone would have broken the filter. Rollo’s and Harald’s decoy mark is a double-bladed axe and the crest carries a single-bladed one. The drawings show the difference plainly, but a name list reading just “axe” lets a team match the decoy by name and never look — which is unfair rather than difficult. It now reads bearded axe, and for the same reason round shield and drinking horn rather than “shield” and “horn”. The horned helmet has no near-match among the nine, so it needs no such guard.
The rule line, and why it is stated outright. Under the title the page reads: “Everything I could find of ours, finally drawn out properly. Every place I actually stopped carries one of these nine.” That second sentence is the filter rule, and it has to be said — without it the drawings are decoration and a team has no reason to compare anything. It says which cards are hers; it does not say which decoys exist, how many there are, or which element sits where.
Two rules for anyone editing the entries later — both are in the script’s header as well.
• A journey entry means the two stops are consecutive, not merely in that order. Naming two stops constrains them whether or not that was the intent, so vet any new entry — including one added purely for texture — against Tools/final_riddle_clues.py before it goes on the sheet.
• Entries are grouped by transport and deliberately not in date order. As printed, the by-train group runs Staraya Ladoga–Kyiv, Walcheren–Châlus, then from Rouen — which is Harald’s leg first and Rollo’s second, the reverse of the real chronology. The list must not give away the order through its own sequence.
2d · Leg 1

Leif — settled

4 cards · digit 1
CardPlaceWhat places it
L1L’Anse aux MeadowsIts own text says it is the first stop of the journey.
L2Battle HarbourElimination — the only real card left once L1 and L3 are fixed.
LDBrattahlíð (decoy)Removed by the crest filter.
L3QikiqtarjuaqThe Leif→Rollo ticket proves it closes the leg.
Leif needs no journal entries at all, so the highlights page does not have to cover the four legs evenly. L1 is the one permitted exception to rule 1. It is handed over at the start and has to launch the game, so “this postcard comes from the first stop on my journey” is framing rather than a puzzle clue. Every other card obeys the rule.

The ambiguity is exactly right. Before the reveal a team knows L1 opens and nothing else: L2, L3 and LD sit in an unknown order. After the reveal LD is gone and the ticket pins L3, so L2 falls into the middle. Both of this leg’s self-numbering sentences have already been deleted — L3’s “Third stop” and L2’s “Second stop” — and both cards are rebuilt.
2e · Leg 2

Rollo — settled

7 cards · digit 9
#CardPlaceWhat places it
1RDWalcheren (decoy)The Leif→Rollo ticket — opens the block.
2R1ChâlusJournal: Walcheren – Châlus.
3R2RouenJournal: Rouen – Bayeux.
4R3BayeuxJournal: Rouen – Bayeux and Bayeux – Winchester.
5R4WinchesterJournal: Bayeux – Winchester, the crossing.
6R5BattleIts own card: “after spending hours looking at the tapestry.”
7R6Roumare ForestThe Rollo→Aud ticket — closes the block.

Three journal entries, and all three are load-bearing — drop any one and the leg admits more than one order. Enumerated over all 5,040 arrangements of the seven cards. The ferry entry does the most work: in its final wording it closes the leg on its own once the two tickets and the other two entries are in hand. The drafted wording of all three is below.

The England-pair inference is not needed. The map shows Winchester and Battle are the two stops across water, which suggests she crossed once and came back. With the three journal entries in place the leg closes whether or not a team makes that leap, so nothing rests on it. It stays available as a confirming read for anyone who spots it — which is the right status for an inference, given rule 3.
Five sentences have to come off Rollo’s built cards. No new prose is invented; each deletion removes a sentence that some prop now does better.

• R1 Châlus — delete “but this is where I began.” Keep “Odd place to start his family’s story, at the very end of it”: that is about Richard the Lionheart dying there, not about her itinerary, so it survives rule 1 and keeps the flavour.
• R6 Roumare — delete “The last of Rollo’s places on my list.” The Rollo→Aud ticket proves it.
• R4 Winchester — delete “Winchester next.” The crossing entry proves it.
• RD Walcheren — delete “before really starting Rollo’s own trail,” which flags the card as a decoy and breaks rule 4.
• R2 Rouen — delete “The tapestry towns are still ahead of me — saving those for later.” Added on 17 Sept and rejected on 20 Sept under rule 3 (see below). The Rouen journal entry now places R2.
• R5 Battle — unchanged. It is the model every other card should be measured against.

All five applied, 20 Sept 2026, with each script carrying a comment recording the rule it answers. PDFs rebuilt and the backs read at full size. R4 now opens “Winchester today”, matching L2’s “Markland today” and H4’s “Today: Hedeby”; R6 keeps the Roumare–Rouen proximity, which is map-verifiable flavour, and loses only the position claim.
Two rejected on the way, and why. R2 Rouen’s “the tapestry towns are still ahead of me” was going to carry R2 < R3, but it breaks rule 3: a solver has to work out that “tapestry towns” means Bayeux, which only the deck itself establishes, and “towns” is ambiguous about whether Battle counts. And a season-based version of this leg — Winchester’s “freezing here” implying November against an October ticket — was dropped for the same reason: it needs outside knowledge of the climate.
Journal · travel highlights

Rollo’s three entries, as drafted

Agreed 20 Sept 2026. These go in the bottom half of the “Family iconography” page, grouped by transport rather than by date. Together with the two transition tickets and Battle’s existing tapestry line they close the leg to one order.

By train
Walcheren – Châlus. The long one. Three changes, one missed connection, and a man in the second carriage who shared his sandwiches after I gave up waiting for the buffet car.
Pins Châlus to second in the block. The only evidence that touches it. Deliberately the warmest of the three, because this is the entry that has to make the decoy read as an ordinary travel memory (rule 4) — missed connections and a stranger’s sandwiches is exactly what someone would write down.
By train
Train from Rouen. Heading west, a short hop, for a museum I’d been looking forward to for months.
Pins Rouen and Bayeux as consecutive — without naming Bayeux. Three readings narrow it together: west and a short hop off the map, and museum excluding Roumare Forest, which is also west of Rouen and far closer. All three are readable from the map and the deck, so the vagueness costs nothing and adds work.
By sea
English Channel ferry. Landed in Winchester after a rocky night, but excited to finally see the battlefield itself, after all those hours in front of the tapestry.
Carries two facts in one clause and closes the leg by itself. “After all those hours in front of the tapestry” puts Bayeux behind her; “excited to finally see the battlefield” puts Battle ahead. The tapestry→Battle link is card-internal — R3 says the tapestry tells of William taking England in 1066, R5 says Battle is where William fought Harold.
Why the final wording points forward rather than back. An earlier draft named Bayeux as the ferry’s origin. The version above is better on two counts. It no longer depends on adjacency — “she went straight from Bayeux to Winchester” was an assumption a team had to make, and now only before-and-after is needed. And it no longer needs the map to rule Châlus out as a Channel port: a fully vague ferry entry leaves two orders, the rival being Châlus → Winchester → Rouen → Bayeux, and closing that gap by reasoning about how far Châlus sits from the sea would have made a soft inference load-bearing.
Proofing note — one misreading inverts the leg. If the ferry entry is read as “excited to see the tapestry” rather than the ground it depicts, Bayeux moves after Winchester and the leg yields three wrong orders. The sentence must make unmistakable that what lies ahead is the place, not the embroidery. The wording above does this twice over — “the battlefield itself” and “after all those hours in front of the tapestry” — and the second half deliberately echoes R3’s own “I spent hours looking at it.” Do not shorten it at layout stage.
Only one of the three names both its endpoints. Walcheren–Châlus does; the Rouen entry withholds its destination and the ferry entry withholds its origin. That is the intended texture — a highlights page should not read as a route table, and the entry that names both ends is the one whose job is to look ordinary.
Leg 2 · dates

Rollo runs 28 Sept – 6 Nov

Set 20 Sept 2026

Dates are a credibility test now, not a clue: under rule 3 no team may be asked to infer anything from a season. They still have to be right, because a card describing a place at an impossible time of year breaks the fiction. Leif’s leg ends 16 Sept, so Rollo opens after that.

The Rouen ticket may not need a date at all. Its intra-leg job disappeared when the Rouen journal entry took over placing R2, and its leg-order job disappeared when the transition-ticket chain took that over. It is therefore a strong candidate to be one of the one or two museum tickets that stay undated — which is the open question in 2f. Nothing in Rollo’s leg breaks if the date comes off.
2f · Leg 3

Aud — settled

4 cards · digit 7

Structurally identical to Leif: four cards, one decoy, both ends pinned by transition tickets. It needs no journal entries at all.

CardPlaceWhat places it
A1DögurðarnesThe Rollo→Aud ticket — opens the leg.
A2Hvammur · museum ticketElimination — the only real card left once A1 and A3 are fixed.
ADBjarnarhöfn (decoy)Removed by the crest filter.
A3EsjubergThe Aud→Harald ticket — closes the leg.
One structural condition: AD must sit second or third in the block, never at either end. If the decoy opened or closed Aud’s leg, the ticket would pin a card that the filter later removes, and the leg would need extra evidence for its real opener or closer — which is exactly the situation Rollo is in, and why Rollo needs a Walcheren–Châlus entry while Aud needs nothing. Note this is not the retired decoy-never-first invariant coming back: it is a per-leg consequence of where that leg’s tickets land, not a rule about decoys in general.

The ambiguity is the same as Leif’s. Before the reveal a team knows nothing about this leg’s order. After it, A1 and A3 are pinned, AD is gone, and A2 falls into the middle.
Four text changes applied to Aud’s built cards, 20 Sept 2026. All four PDFs are rebuilt and the backs visually checked.

• A1 Dögurðarnes — deleted the whole opening paragraph, “First of Aud’s places for me. I saved Aud’s country until after France — save the best for last, right?” The first sentence states the card’s own position (rule 1). The second was the more serious of the two: it reveals Rollo-before-Aud during play, when leg order is supposed to live entirely in the final bundle (rule 5). The card now opens on the “meal headland” paragraph.
• A3 Esjuberg — deleted “Last of Aud’s places for me.” (rule 1). Opens on Helgi now.
• AD Bjarnarhöfn — “Wintered like Aud did, at her brother’s harbor” became “At her brother’s harbor — the one Aud wintered in.” Not a rules fix but a credibility one: Aud’s leg now runs May–July, so Liv cannot have wintered there. The reworded line echoes Aud’s winter instead of claiming Liv’s, and agrees with the card’s own typed Fun Fact, which already says Aud wintered there before founding Hvammur.
• The A1 and A3 deletions are the two clauses the 17 Sept clue set listed as “new card text” still to write. They had in fact already been written and shipped — worth remembering when auditing Harald, whose equivalent clauses on H1 and H6 are listed the same way.
A1 lost its warm opener and has not gained a replacement. The card now begins straight into the etymology. That reads acceptably — it matches R3 Bayeux, which also opens on its subject — but if it feels abrupt at proof stage, any replacement must carry no ordering content at all. Left deliberately open rather than invented.
Not yet decided for this leg: its dates inside the May–July window, and where exactly AD sits (second or third — either satisfies the condition above). Aud’s museum ticket is A2 Hvammur, which under PZ-17 also carries the treasure-hunt code and the seven-row route-record form; whether it keeps a printed date is part of the open museum-ticket question.
2g · Leg 4

One journal entry closes it. The Aud→Harald ticket, H6’s own line and H4’s Mediterranean line leave just two possible orders — H1 H2 H3 H4 H5 H6 and H1 H3 H2 H4 H5 H6 — so the only open question on this leg is whether Staraya Ladoga or Kyiv came first. A single entry settles it.

Corrected, 20 Sept 2026 — only the real cards need ordering. An earlier pass on this leg called for two entries, the second being Sicily – Constantinople. That was an error of method: it was enumerating all seven cards of the block, when HD’s position inside the block is never needed by anything. It is filtered out before a single line is drawn, no ticket lands on it, and the decoy-never-first invariant is retired. Enumerating the six real cards instead drops the requirement from two entries to one.

The same simplification does not help Rollo, and the contrast is worth keeping. Rollo’s decoy is genuinely load-bearing: the Leif→Rollo ticket lands on RD Walcheren rather than on a real card, so Walcheren – Châlus is what converts “the block starts at Walcheren” into “Châlus is the first real stop.” Re-enumerated over Rollo’s six real cards: all three of its entries survive, and dropping any one gives three orders.
Journal · travel highlights

Harald’s one entry

By train
Staraya Ladoga – Kyiv. Two days on a train, most of it running alongside the rivers they would have rowed. Felt like cheating. Slept through the best of it, naturally.
Pins Staraya Ladoga and Kyiv as consecutive, which is the leg’s only open question. The Varangian river route is the obvious thing to remember about this journey, so the entry needs no excuse to exist.

Deliberately no direction word. An early draft said she followed the water “south”, which would have muddied H4’s “from here I’m turning south” — a team could reasonably wonder whether the turn had already happened. The rivers carry the same flavour without the ambiguity.

Why the H4 wording matters more than it looks. The card now reads: “Today: Hedeby — barely a Hedeby left to stand in, just a green ring of earthworks where the ramparts ran. From here I’m turning south: nothing but Mediterranean weather for the rest of this trip.”

It replaced “saltwater again, after all that river country”, which was doing the same job badly. “River country” only reliably points at Staraya Ladoga, whose card mentions a river bend; Kyiv’s card mentions only hills, so placing Kyiv before Hedeby needed outside knowledge of the Dnieper.

And a plain “heading south” would not have worked either. Hedeby sits at 54.5°N and Kyiv at 50.5°N — Kyiv is south of Hedeby, so a latitude reading cannot exclude it and the clue says nothing useful. Naming the sea does: Kyiv is the one remaining stop not on a coast at all. The difference is 60 possible orders versus 4, and one journal entry saved. “Weather” reads as flavour here — the anchor a team actually uses is the named sea, checked against the map, not a claim about climate.
Two text changes applied, 20 Sept 2026; both PDFs rebuilt and the backs visually checked.
• H4 Hedeby — the opener replaced as above. Its branch-rune key table was the risk: that box clamps at y = 30 and an earlier edit to this card pushed its caption out of frame. The new message runs one line longer and the table still clears, caption intact — confirmed by rendering the back at full size, not by arithmetic.
• H1 Oslo — deleted “and the obvious place to begin with him” (rule 1). The line now reads “It’s Harald’s city — so much Viking history.” The Aud→Harald ticket proves H1 opens the leg. Note this card’s source uses straight apostrophes where the rest of the deck uses curly ones — worth normalising at some point.
• H6 Patara — kept. “Patara is the last stop of my travels for now” is load-bearing: no ticket follows Harald, so the card has to close the chain.
Applied, 20 Sept 2026 — H3 Kyiv rewritten. The card opened “Kyiv next…”, which broke rule 2. It now reads “Kyiv, and it might be the most beautiful city on this whole trip — golden domes catching the light on every hill.” Nothing replaces it mechanically, and nothing needed to: an earlier plan had H3 gaining a Dnieper mention so “river country” would cover it, and H4’s Mediterranean line made that unnecessary. The new opener carries no ordering content at all. The rest of the card — St Michael’s, the candle, the HONEY riddle — is untouched. H1’s deletion is applied too; H6 keeps its line by design.
Decided, 20 Sept 2026 — the 2 stands as it is. The traced figure is rough: the H3 → H4 leg doubles back up and to the left, so it reads closer to a zigzag with a spike than to a clean 2. Recorded as a problem since 17 Sept and now accepted rather than fixed. The reasoning is that by this point a team knows it is looking for a digit, and a rough 2 is still the only digit that figure resembles — recognition is much easier than reading a shape cold. Fixing it would have meant moving or replacing a stop, which in turn invalidates whichever clues named it. This was the last open problem in the endgame.
2h · What this model still owes

Open, and in rough order of who blocks whom

Legs 3–4 not started

All four legs closed 20 Sept 2026

Leif and Aud need no journal entries; Rollo needs three and Harald one. Every leg has been enumerated exhaustively and each surviving clue is load-bearing. What remains is production, not design: the props do not exist yet, and the solver and solving board still encode the superseded 17 Sept model.

Which museum tickets keep a date open

One or two of the four lose their dates so the set cannot be sorted early. None of the four carries a printed date today, so this is a decision rather than a correction. Rouen is the leading candidate to go undated — both of its jobs have been taken over by other props, so nothing in Rollo’s leg breaks without it.

The journal page built 20 Sept 2026

Both halves exist and print: Props/Journal/build_journal_page_pdf.py, A5 with a Letter version. Four entries, all load-bearing. Vet every future entry with the enumerator before it goes on the sheet — including any added purely for texture, because naming two stops constrains them whether or not that was the intent.

Dates year split decided

The journey spans two years. Leif 24 Aug – 16 Sept and Rollo 28 Sept – 6 Nov in year one; Aud and Harald in May–July of year two. This replaces a continuous run that would have pushed legs 3 and 4 into an Icelandic and Baltic winter. Seasonal reasoning is not a clue under rule 3, but it still governs whether a card’s content is credible. Exact dates inside the May–July window are open.

Solver and board rebuilt, 20 Sept 2026. Tools/final_riddle_clues.py and the solving board in section 6 now encode this model and agree with each other. The script got simpler in the process: blocked legs are independent, so each is enumerated exhaustively over its own block — at most 7! = 5,040 arrangements — instead of searching 22 global positions under a 400,000 cap. A pass can no longer be an artefact of the cap, which the old model produced at least once. final_riddle_solver.py is retained but marked superseded and is no longer imported.
Visuals regenerated, 20 Sept 2026. The itinerary table in section 2, the two timelines and the four leg maps all come from Tools/build_final_riddle_visuals.py, which now carries the blocked-leg dates. The traced digits are unchanged — stop coordinates never moved — but every decoy has a new position, and two of the old shape readings changed with it. The script’s output path is also no longer a dead reference to one session’s temp directory.
Nothing in this tab is stale as of 20 Sept 2026. The last two blocks that predated the evidence model — “clue types and their jobs” and the per-trail constraint worksheet — have been retired rather than patched, with a note saying what they claimed and why it is wrong; see section 3. The one column of the worksheet that had not moved, the open element-mark assignment, is carried forward in the summary table that replaced it.
2i · At a glance

The year, the cards and the four shapes

Generated from the itinerary

All three drawings are generated from the recorded itinerary and from the same coordinates the printed trail maps use (TravelMap/stops.js), by NorseBackpack/Tools/build_final_riddle_visuals.py. Re-run it after changing a date or a stop so the pictures cannot drift from the table above.

Where she was, and when

Twenty-two stops on one line, positioned by real date, coloured by leg. Each leg now runs start to finish before the next begins, so the colours no longer interleave — the blocked-leg model, visible. The long gap is the winter she spends abroad between Rollo and Aud, and the marked January is the year boundary. 79 nights away, 24 Aug 2024 to 21 Jul 2025.

Which trail is live, and when

The same axis, one lane per leg. This is the blocked-leg model at a glance — each lane is one unbroken run, and no leg is ever interrupted by another. It supersedes the interleaved year, where every trail except Leif was cut across by a second one, and it is why the four legs can now be solved independently.

This is the date each card was sent, not the order players receive them. The in-game release order is PC-18 and is still open — Leif’s, Aud’s and Harald’s legs have a recorded lock chain in Puzzles & locks, but Rollo’s three puzzles still have no internal order, so a release-order version of this chart cannot be drawn without inventing one.

The four shapes, and where each decoy sits

Numbered in travel order, north-up Web Mercator, uniform scale per panel so no shape is distorted. Solid line = the real route and the digit it draws. Dashed rust = what a team would trace if it failed to filter the decoy out. Dots sit at true position; only the number badges are pushed apart, with a leader back to the dot.

What these panels show, read honestly. They are a first look at the question the solver cannot answer, not a verdict — the shapes still need checking at true size on the laminated sheets. Regenerated 20 Sept 2026 from the blocked-leg dates; the traced digits are unchanged, because the stop coordinates never moved, but every decoy has a new position and two of the readings below changed with it.

• Rollo’s 9 is the strongest of the four. Tail at the bottom, closed loop on top. It works because R6 Roumare lands 3.6 px from R2 Rouen on this panel, so the loop genuinely closes — the same near-coincidence that killed the pin-and-cord idea (PZ-06) is what makes the digit read.
• Aud’s 7 reads, but its descender is vertical rather than slanted, because Esjuberg sits almost due south of Hvammur. Acceptable, not ideal.
• Leif’s 1 is effectively a two-point line. L1 and L2 are 7.6 px apart against a 224 px trail, so the shape is a single stroke. A 1 does not need internal shape, so this is not a problem to solve.
• Harald’s 2 is rough and is being kept. The H3 → H4 leg doubles back, so the figure reads closer to a zigzag with a spike. Accepted 20 Sept 2026 rather than fixed: by this point a team knows it is looking for a digit, and recognition is far easier than reading a shape cold. See 2g.

Both decoy complaints from 17 Sept are now resolved — by the re-dating, not by design work. The old note recorded that two decoys barely perturbed their shape, so a team that never worked out the crest filter still traced usable digits. Brattahlíð no longer “adds a hook”: it sat last in Leif’s run and now sits third of four, so an unfiltered trace runs L1 → L2 → east to Greenland → back west to Baffin — a V, which is not a 1. And Walcheren now opens Rollo’s block rather than sitting late in it, so an unfiltered trace starts far to the north-east and the loop never closes. Constantinople still sits almost on the line between Sicily and Patara, so Harald’s decoy remains the weak one of the four — the only case where ignoring the filter still leaves a plausible figure.

Resolved, 20 Sept 2026 — H5 is Aci Castello everywhere. See the note below the panels.
Resolved, 20 Sept 2026 — the H5 Aci Castello / Syracuse split. Three artefacts had been disagreeing three ways: TravelMap/stops.js and the route plan said Syracuse at 37.069, 15.288 with a sourced Harald link; the visuals script said “Aci Castello” at Syracuse’s coordinates, which was incoherent; and the card carries Aci Castello front art, an ACI CASTELLO postmark and a message about its Norman castle.

Decided: Aci Castello everywhere, at its real coordinates 37.5545, 15.1462. All three files now agree and every downstream artefact is regenerated — the two timelines, the four leg panels, and all eight printed trail map PDFs. The choice was made against moving the stop to Syracuse, which would have cost new front art, a new postmark and a rewritten card, and against leaving it inconsistent.

The geometry does not care. The two sites are 55 km apart, which moves H5 about 4 px on a 224 px panel. Harald’s 2 is unchanged.

What the choice costs, recorded rather than hidden. This is the one Harald stop with no Harald connection. His Sicilian service is attested at Syracuse and Messina under George Maniakes in 1038–1040, not here, and the castle on the lava rock is Norman, built around 1076 — a decade after Harald died. Sicily is on the trail because of the Maniakes campaign; this particular town is simply where Liv stayed. The stop’s evidence tier in stops.js has therefore been dropped from supported to illustrative, and the Syracuse citation kept in place to explain why Sicily is on the trail at all.

One note from the map build worth keeping: Aci Castello plots 0.2 km into the sea against the 50 m coastline, which is 0.0 pt at print scale — the castle really does sit on a rock at the shoreline. Patara is 1.8 km out, 0.4 pt. Neither is visible.
3 · Retired

Clue types, and the per-trail constraint worksheet

Retired 20 Sept 2026
Two blocks have been retired here rather than patched. Both predate the evidence model in 2c and described jobs the props no longer do. Patching them would have produced something half-true; the current answers are in 2c–2g, and the summary below carries the one column that is still live.

• “Clue types and their jobs” (four panels) said museum tickets were calendar anchors dated 2 Feb / 17 Mar / 13 Apr / 28 Apr, that postcard statements “carry the whole deduction”, and that the bundle held two or three cross-trail resolvers. All three are now wrong. Leg order moved to the three transition tickets; the museum tickets carry no ordering at all and one or two will be deliberately undated; and postcard statements are reduced to two natural cross-references, because a card may no longer state its own position or point at another card.
• The per-trail constraint worksheet listed decoy positions from the interleaved year (“pos. 11, June”, “pos. 20, Nov”) that no longer exist, ticket dates that have all changed, and an order column built on “Winchester next” and “Kyiv next” — the two clues this model removes outright.
Summary

The four legs at a glance

Element marks assigned · see 3b

What replaces the worksheet: only the columns that are still true. The element-mark column was the one piece of the old table that had not moved. It closed on 20 Sept 2026 — all 22 cards now carry their mark and are rebuilt; the assignment is in 3b.

Fourteen clues in total, every one load-bearing. The two four-card legs close on the tickets alone; the journal is what closes the two six-card legs.

Superseded, 20 Sept 2026 — see rule 4 in 2c. This invariant existed because leg order came from each trail’s first appearance, so a filtered-out decoy made “when did this trail start?” ambiguous. The transition-ticket chain now carries leg order, so a decoy may open its block — and RD Walcheren does. The original note follows.

New structural rule, 17 Sept 2026: a decoy is never its own trail’s earliest card. If it were, “first appearance” would be ambiguous — players would not know whether a card they have just filtered out still counts as the trail appearing. All four decoys above sit well inside their trail. This tested as individually redundant in one clue set and load-bearing in another, so it is recorded as an invariant rather than a clue.
3b · The filter, on the cards

Element marks assigned and printed

Applied 20 Sept 2026 · all 22

The last open column of the retired worksheet is now closed. Every card carries its mark, every card is rebuilt, and the crest filter exists on paper for the first time.

The rule the assignment had to satisfy. Each three-stop leg (Leif, Aud) uses the symbol set — raven, longship, sun-wheel — each element exactly once. Each six-stop leg (Rollo, Harald) uses the crest set — battle-axe, round shield, wolf, anchor, drinking horn, valknut — each element exactly once. Every decoy carries a mark from neither set.

Any bijection satisfying that works identically as a puzzle, so the pairings below are chosen for flavour alone and can be reshuffled freely. They live in a single dict in NorseBackpack/Postcards/postcard_marks.py, which also carries a check() that asserts the rule still holds and that every image file exists — run it after any change. No clue depends on which element sits where.
