Placement: the top right, on all 22 — revised 20 Sept 2026. The mark sits in the pocket between the divider and the postmark, above the address box, at the same coordinates on every card.

The first attempt put it beside the signature and that was wrong. It was free space, but it was free space in the message half, among Liv’s own margin drawings — on R3 the round shield read as a fourth piece of the Bayeux rebus, and on H4 it crowded the branch-rune key. Up in the top right the mark is unmistakably part of the card’s printed furniture rather than something she drew, and the rebus on R3 is left alone in the message half where it belongs.

The position is free by construction, not by luck. The divider (x 184), the address box (topping out at y 188) and the postmark circle (centred 278, 204, r 23) are at identical coordinates on all 22 cards — verified across every build script — so the pocket at roughly x 186–253 by y 190–220 exists everywhere. It is defined once as MARK_X, MARK_Y in postcard_marks.py; every card now calls draw_mark(c, "XX") with no coordinates, so moving them all again is a one-line change.
Mostly answered by the move, 20 Sept 2026. The marks and the rebuses do share a visual language — small hand-drawn icons, same palette, same hand — and while the marks sat beside the signature that was a real risk of misreading, worst on R3. Moving them to the top right separates them by zone rather than by style: her drawings live in the message half, the element marks live with the printed furniture. What still wants a real playtest is whether a team notices the marks at all up there, since they now compete with the stamp and postmark for attention rather than sitting where the eye already is.
4 · Guessing risks

The shape has to carry the real difficulty

Design risk. A traced shape reads the same forwards and backwards, and 3-point trails barely constrain the drawing, so the real difficulty has to sit in Rollo, Harald and the order of the last three digits. For each 6-stop trail, alternative orders that survive the pre-bundle clues should draw other plausible digits (a 9 versus a 5 or 0; a 2 versus a 5 or Z). Candidate code 1972 reads as a year, which self-checks but also invites year-guessing. Harald’s 2 currently reads as a zigzag (PZ-09), which weakens the shape as a self-check.
5 · Validation

By script, not by eye

Re-run 20 Sept 2026
Current run, 20 Sept 2026 — blocked-leg model, exhaustive.
LegBlockCards only+ tickets+ journal
Leif4211
Rollo7360601
Aud4611
Harald7621
TEST 1 every leg has one answer — pass. TEST 2 the journal gates — pass; without it Rollo admits 60 orders and Harald 2. TEST 3 no decoy reads as a shape — not run, still geometric. TEST 4 leg order is no longer searched: the three transition tickets chain it directly.

All fourteen clues are load-bearing — the script drops each in turn and none is redundant. Note the two four-card legs are closed by the tickets alone; the journal is what closes the two seven-card legs. The paragraph below describes the retired 17 Sept run and is kept for its two findings, which still hold as cautions.

Superseded. The solver lived at NorseBackpack/Tools/final_riddle_solver.py with its clue set in final_riddle_clues.py. It encodes every clue as a constraint over the 22 cards and 22 sequence positions and enumerates valid orderings. Re-run it whenever a clue, a card’s text or the itinerary changes.

1 · Unique answer pass

Exactly one answer survives, where “answer” means the order within each trail plus first-appearance order. Proven analytically — each trail’s order is forced by its clues — and confirmed over the first 400,000 valid calendars. The full calendar is not unique and does not need to be: cross-trail interleaving is almost entirely free, so many calendars give the same answer.

2 · Bundle actually gates pass

Without the bundle, 3 answers survive: H5 Aci Castello floats inside Harald’s leg. The bundle’s Hamburg → Catania boarding pass is the single ordering resolver. The crest/logo note in the same bundle is the absolute gate — the solver assumes filtering has already happened, so without that note the endgame cannot begin at all.

3 · No decoy reads as a shape not run

This one is geometric, not logical — it needs each decoy city plotted on its trail’s real map projection to see what digit the wrong route draws. The solver cannot answer it. Still open under PZ-18, together with the tension that Guessing risks above wants a decoy to draw a plausible wrong digit while this test wants it to draw none.

4 · Rules hold pass

First appearances run Leif, Rollo, Aud, Harald, at positions 1, 4, 6 and 8. The old “every stated gap is a multiple of 3 days” rule is retired with the slot grid — no clue in the current set states a gap in days at all, so the rule has nothing to constrain.

Two findings worth keeping from the solver runs. First, individually redundant clues are not jointly redundant: six clues each tested as droppable on their own, and removing all six together took the answer count from 1 to 8. Never prune the clue table on drop-one results alone. Second, two clue-set shapes were tried and rejected on evidence — moving Rollo’s closing clue into the bundle as a dated receipt (17 answers; a date pins R6’s position but not its rank, so R3–R5 can still follow it), and resolving Harald’s tail from the bundle alone with no added card clause (6 answers).
This supersedes the postmark-date endgame and the plan to hold back Rollo, Aud and Harald cards (PZ-11, now superseded — decoys provide the same early-drawing protection, provided each decoy draws a plausible wrong digit). Postmarks still identify the place, but they are not the ordering key. Decided as of 17 Sept 2026: the decoy cities, the symbol/crest elements, the full itinerary with its dates, and the clue set. Still open under PZ-18: per-stop element-mark assignment, the element mark’s physical form and its legibility test, the remaining final-bundle contents, and validation test 3.
6 · Solving board

Place the deck, tick the clues, watch them turn

Rebuilt 20 Sept 2026

A working surface for playtesting the endgame, not a player-facing prop. Drag a postcard from the deck into a slot, or click a card and then click a slot. Click a clue row to tick it off as used — that mark is yours and nothing sets it for you. The verdict chip on the right of each row is automatic: green when your arrangement satisfies that clue, red when it breaks it, grey until enough cards are down to judge. Your board is kept in this browser between visits; Clear board empties it.

Rebuilt for the blocked-leg model, 20 Sept 2026. The first board was a single row of 22 positions, because the four trails were interleaved across one year. They are not any more: Liv finishes each leg before starting the next, so the board is now four separate blocks and each is judged on its own. The clue list is the current one from 2c–2g — fourteen clues, down from nineteen — and the two “next” clues the old board showed are gone. It holds the same data as Tools/final_riddle_clues.py; change one and change the other.

What it does not do: it checks one arrangement, it does not enumerate, so it cannot prove uniqueness. That is the script’s job, and unlike the old one the script now runs exhaustively rather than under a cap.

The deck

Twenty-two cards — eighteen real stops and one decoy per leg. Colour is the stamp, so it is the leg; the decoys are tinted.

The four legs

Each leg runs start to finish before the next begins, so each block is judged on its own. Shaded slots are a block’s first and last — the two a transition ticket or a permitted card statement pins.

The clues

All fourteen, and the prop each one lives on. Click a row to tick it off as used.

Approved update, 22 September 2026: coin values total 3705 and rotate to SOLE; HRAFN selects four flight digits, 2648, for a lock directly on the board pouch. The scale and cryptex are superseded. Full specifications, visual and postcard changes. Older options below retain their historical status.
Core mechanic · work in progress

The postcard system

Eighteen postcards as the game itself, with every other prop reduced to an instrument that acts on them. This tab is the current thinking, not a finished spec — the parts that are not settled are marked, and the full list lives in Open questions.

Status. The shape below is agreed; the contents are not. The three jobs, the two voices and the verb list are the working model. The card-by-card assignment is a first pass with one acknowledged gap, and the eighteen dates, captions and digits are all unwritten.
The brief

A card does three jobs

1 · It is a map point

The stamp motif gives the trail; a small element mark gives whether the card counts toward that trail's shape; the postmark gives the place only. All 22 feed the final reveal: split by stamp, filter out the four decoys with the crest/logo note, reconstruct the order from ticket/postcard/final-bundle evidence, draw the legs, read the four digits. Candidate — see Final riddle tab

2 · It is worth reading

Real trivia about a real place, in a titled Fun Fact block (was the printed caption — renamed and moved when the address block shrank to make room for it). Written on card L1 (the butternut wood found far from any butternut tree) — the template for the other seventeen.

3 · It carries a clue

Alone or in a group. Her message sets a task, or four cards have to be physically lined up before they say anything at all.

Every card is active. All 22 do a job besides the final ordering, including the four decoys. This replaces the earlier “five or six active cards, the rest quiet” decision, which is why the sparse-channel worksheet has been removed from the Design spec tab.
Decided, 15 Sept 2026 — every card's job 3 gates a lock. Sharpens the rule above: a card's clue can't just be flavour, a calendar statement, or an element mark on its own — it has to feed an actual physical lock, the same standard cards L1–L3 and Rouen/Bayeux/Winchester/Battle already meet. This applies to decoys too: Brattahlíð already does (it carries the real beasts-chain imprint), Walcheren now does too, alongside Châlus and Roumare — all three combine into the "counting in the illustration" lock (PZ-19) — and Bjarnarhöfn now does too, paired with the comb to gate entry to Harald's leg (PZ-17). Currently short of this standard: Constantinople, the one still-unassigned decoy.
The craft idea

Every card carries two voices

This is how real picture postcards work, and it is what lets jobs 2 and 3 sit on one small piece of card without either getting in the other’s way.

Fun Fact, typed · the publisher’s
L’ANSE AUX MEADOWS, NEWFOUNDLAND — The only confirmed Norse settlement in North America, excavated 1961–1968 by Helge and Anne Stine Ingstad. Eight timber-and-sod structures. Édition Vinland Nº 14.
Job: facts, and the numbers that hide in them. Dry, third-person, small type in the titled Fun Fact block, on all 18 — nobody suspects a caption.
Handwritten · hers
Third time here and the wind still finds the one gap in my coat. Don’t believe the eight structures — I counted seven and an argument. Ask your father about the comb.
Job: voice, tasks, and the rules. Which cards, in what order, what to do. The quirk lives entirely here.

The split does quiet work. A typed Fun Fact can carry a number without ever looking planted, because it is a genuine fact about a genuine place. And because she argues with it in her own handwriting elsewhere on the card, she can point at exactly the number she wants read without ever writing “use this number.”

Decided. Fun Fact is always typed, on every card — never handwritten. Keeps the two voices doing one job each: typed for facts and hidden numbers, her handwriting for voice, tasks and rules. PC-12 closed.

The figures in the sample caption above are illustrative and have not been source-checked. See Props & specs for the verification rule.

The toolbox

What a deck of cards can do that a sheet of clues cannot

If the postcards are the game, these are the game’s verbs. Each is physical, each is resettable, and each costs nothing beyond print and a pair of scissors.

A
Line four up and read across the seams
Her sentence runs off the right edge of one card and continues on the next. Self-verifying — the sentence either reads or it does not, so no lock is needed to confirm it. Worked out below.
B
Hold two together against a lamp
Print one card’s back on lighter stock; overlaid on another and lit from behind, marks from both combine into a third thing. The same instinct as a lithophane, at no cost.
C
Lay the comb across a message
The grille already in the design. Broken teeth expose certain words; rotated or flipped, one comb reads several cards. Reuse is the point — one prop across two stages is harder for players and cheaper to build.
D
Argue with the Fun Fact
Her handwriting contradicts the typed facts. Points at a number without saying “use this number,” and it is genuinely funny.
E
Set a task in the message
The card is the rule card. “The game we used to play — I have set it up as I left it. King to a corner in three. You never could see the second move.”
F
Fold one card
A crease brings two halves of a message together to form a sentence present in neither half. Needs a card you are willing to crease, so use it once and print a spare.
G
Sort, group and order
The endgame, and the one verb the whole deck performs together. Split by stamp, order by date, draw the legs on the four maps.
Worth building first. It proves the format to the players. Once a group has physically pushed two postcards together and watched a sentence appear, they will try it with every card in the deck for the rest of the game — which answers “where do I look?” for free on every later grouping puzzle, and turns the cards from pictures into components.
First pass · not final

The honest gap. Family D has four cards and no selection rule. Every other family is selected by something physical or something she wrote; D currently is not selected by anything. The props, re-cast

If the cards are the game, every prop’s job is now to act on a card. The comb reads a message; the magnifier reads a caption; the balance orders three cards; the board is set up by a card; the red filter and the UV pen reveal a layer on a card. The rune stick and the cryptex are the only things that never touch the deck — which is fine, since they are the one pair that should feel as though they came from somewhere else entirely. Superseded, 22 Sept 2026: there is no balance or cryptex any more — the coin tally (G7) and the raven’s flights perch card (G8) replace them; see Approved changes.

Superseded, 16 Sept 2026

Interleaved dates

Postmarks carry no date at all now. This whole mechanism — arranging eighteen postmark dates so the trails overlap instead of sorting cleanly — is dropped. Order is reconstructed from ticket dates, postcard statements and the final bundle instead (PZ-18, Final riddle tab), never from anything printed on a card. See PC-02/PC-03/PC-04. The reasoning below is kept for reference, not the current plan.

She crisscrossed rather than finishing one leg before starting the next. Seventeen of the eighteen dates are still unwritten; this decides the shape they take.

Settle before writing the dates. Eighteen interleaved dates across a long trip need a year, or at least an unambiguous season. Card L1 currently reads 07 JUL with no year — fine alone, ambiguous once the trails overlap. Decide the span first. Tracked as PC-03.

Moved from the Design spec, 17 Sept 2026

Format, count and address

One card per route stop, giving 18 cards across the four trails. At this volume the cards stop being individual clues and become a corpus, which is a better toy: it supports grouping, sorting and a much larger final reveal.

Partly superseded. The sparse-channel model below — a quiet majority of cards with data on a few — has been replaced by the all-active model in the Postcard system tab, which is the current spec. What stays true here is the count, size, format, address, stamp-series idea and the mechanics list. The anomaly and tilted-stamp channels are dropped.
DecisionValue

SizeApprox. 5 × 3.5 in (vintage postcard proportion)

FormatPurchased but never sent — addressed and stamped like a real postcard, just not mailed

Format: purchased, never sent

Each card is addressed and stamped like a real postcard rather than a plain note, which is what makes an unmailed stamp collection plausible in the first place: travellers buy postcards and never send half of them. It also earns back an address block as usable clue space, which a note-card format would give up for nothing.

The recipient is fictional: John Ericson, 24 Longship Way, Ottawa ON K1L 1S1, Canada. "Longship Way" doesn't exist as an Ottawa street, so the address can't collide with a real occupied building regardless of the postal code. Ericson doubles as a quiet nod to Leif without being tied to his trail specifically — it reads as the family surname.

The same stamp series is reused across countries within a trail; explained, if it ever needs explaining, as the aunt buying a batch of stamps at the first post office of each leg. This is ordinary traveller behaviour and doesn't need to be stated in-fiction.

First-card layout, built: the writing side is split vertically, with Aunt Liv's existing message on the left and, on the right, the Leif longship stamp and postmark, a shrunk boxed address, and a boxed, titled Fun Fact block underneath it — matching the current spec in the Postcard system tab. The stamp sits at roughly 20 × 24 mm. Superseded, 16 Sept 2026: the circular mark's date half (07 JUL, with an underlined day left over from the superseded 0734 mechanism) is dropped entirely — postmarks carry the place only now (PC-02/PC-03/PC-04). Card L1's back still needs rebuilding to drop the date; the mark itself also still needs redrawing as a real cancel rather than a clean graphic (PC-10).

Parked idea. One postcard could carry a different recipient address as a "look here" signal. Still parked, and now the only surviving piece of the dropped anomaly idea — with every card active there is nothing left for such a signal to select, so it would need a new job before it is worth using.

Superseded: the sparse-channel model

This section used to specify a quiet majority of cards with data carried on small subsets — four price cards, five extra-text cards, four tilted stamps and three anomaly stamps — together with the card-by-card worksheet that assigned them.

It has been replaced by the all-active model: every one of the 18 cards does a job besides the final ordering, the data lives in a titled Fun Fact block rather than in a sparse channel, and the anomaly and tilted-stamp channels are dropped. The reasoning and the new assignment are in the Postcard system tab.

Kept as a note rather than deleted silently, because the argument it made — that scarcity is itself a signal — is sound and may be worth reviving if the deck ever grows past 18 cards.

Other postcard mechanics worth using

  • Grille overlay. A card with windows cut in it laid over a message, exposing only certain words. One grille rotated or flipped decodes several cards. See the comb prop below.
  • Hole-punch stack. Punched holes plus a registration notch; stacked in the right order against a window, only certain letters on the bottom card stay visible. The order is the puzzle, and it rehearses the skill the final map needs.
  • Edge continuation. Horizon lines that continue between cards, with something written across the seams and legible only when the joint is closed.
  • Fold lines. A crease brings two halves of a message together to form a sentence present in neither half.
  • Counting in the illustration. Boats, birds, windows. Trivial on its own — only ship it with a non-obvious ordering rule attached.
Artwork consequence of the size change. Done for cards L1–L3: the illustration sources and generated fronts are 1500 × 1050 px (10:7), reframed without aspect distortion. The remaining production change is in build_postcard_collection.py, where SIZE and PAGE must change together before cards L2/L3 and the full collection PDF are rebuilt.
Approved update, 22 September 2026: coin values total 3705 and rotate to SOLE; HRAFN selects four flight digits, 2648, for a lock directly on the board pouch. The scale and cryptex are superseded. Full specifications, visual and postcard changes. Older options below retain their historical status.
Every decided puzzle, its clues and its lock

Puzzles & locks

One place for every puzzle that has been worked out: the lock it feeds, the code it produces, and links straight to the props and postcards that carry its clues. Status pills say how settled each one is — decided, partly decided, open. Every entry carries the open-question IDs it is tracked under; the full reasoning and anything still unresolved lives in Open questions, which stays the single backlog.

There is no decided lock order across the whole game. Container structure is deliberately unfrozen (ST-01), so several chains end in “open” — the puzzle is settled, what it physically opens is not.

The standard every puzzle here is held to

Moved here from the Design spec, 17 Sept 2026 — it is a rule for writing puzzles, so it belongs with the puzzles.

The Hiking backpack is the calibration. Its codes sit at the intersection of two or three props, its items arrive before their purpose is known, and the part it hides is the ordering rule rather than the data. Norse should be a notch harder than that.

The three questions

Run every puzzle through these:

  • Where do I look?
  • What do I take from it?
  • In what order?

Answering all three makes the puzzle obvious. Answering none makes it unfair. Answer two, hide one. The hidden one should almost always be the third.

Hide the rule, not the data

Numbers and letters stay in plain sight. What players have to earn is which ones to use and in what sequence. This is what the Hiking sticker puzzle does: the digits are visible, the historical-era ordering is the puzzle.

Re-read, don't re-find

The cheapest strong aha is re-reading a prop already read for a different reason. Plant ordering rules as throwaway habits in the aunt's letters, then make one of them load-bearing three puzzles later.

Two or three props per code

At least two codes in the game should need three separate props: a data source, a selection rule and an ordering rule. This is the main difficulty lever and it is cheaper than adding puzzles.

Answers self-verify

Where possible the final step should produce a word or a shape that is visibly right before the lock is touched. Hiking does this with MOON.

Soft red herrings only

Nothing is a dead end. A prop that looks irrelevant now must be load-bearing later. Never spend a player's attention without eventually repaying it.

Supply every table

No outside knowledge, ever. Rune tables, coin references and board rules all ship inside the bag, the way Hiking ships a periodic table and a calculator.

Hint ladder. Hiking runs two hints per stage. Three-prop codes need three: the first purely locational ("you already have everything — one piece is a card you have already read"), the second naming the props, the third naming the rule.

Everything at a glance

Full mechanisms, grouped by trail

Puzzle by puzzle

Parked

Ideas, not actioned yet

Worked-up puzzles and answers with no trail, no lock and no container. Nothing here is committed. Move one out of this list only when it has actually been decided, and record the decision in the table above.

Live register

Open questions

The single place where unresolved design questions live. Every question has a stable ID so chat, HANDOFF.md and this page can refer to the same thing. If a question is not on this list it is not tracked; if it is resolved, the entry changes to Decided rather than disappearing.

Priority · moved from the Design spec, 17 Sept 2026

Do these next, in this order

Nothing below needs the backpack to be bought first. Every item is one of the IDs listed below on this tab — this block only says which order to take them in.

  1. Build the continuation set on paper

    Four cards, one sentence, scissors. It proves the format, and if it does not feel good in the hand nothing else in the postcard system is worth doing.

  2. Write card L1 properly Done

    Fun Fact written (butternuts, far from any butternut tree), the message kept as-is (PC-08 decided), and the back rebuilt to the new layout. It's the template the other seventeen get written against.

  3. Rebuild card L1's back to drop its postmark date

    PC-02. Postmarks now carry place only, no date at all (PC-03/PC-04, removed) — L1 is the only built card that still prints one (07 JUL).

  4. Solve family D

    PC-05. Give those four cards a selection rule as good as the others, or redistribute them and drop the family.

  5. Write the 18 Fun Facts

    PC-07. Real trivia, source-checked, with the numbers you need already living in them.

  6. Print-proof the revised Harald and Aud stamps

    HI-01 and HI-02 are redrawn: Aud now uses the comb and Harald a single-bladed Dane axe. Check both at the finished 20×24 mm size before the full deck print.

  7. Finish the postcard collection size conversion

    PC-11. The three fronts are done at 1500 × 1050 px. Change SIZE and PAGE together in build_postcard_collection.py, then rebuild cards L2/L3 and the full collection PDF.

  8. Run the print and filter tests

    PR-03 to PR-05, plus a full 18-card print to check the table footprint against four maps.

  9. Source-check the history

    HI-05. Everything in Historical claims needing verification, at the foot of this tab, before any of it reaches a prop.

Open questions are the normal state here. This is a work in progress and not everything needs solving at once. A session usually moves one or two of these, and adding a new question is as valid an outcome as closing one.
ST

Structure and containers

ST-01 Direction set · count open
How many containers is the bag divided into, and what are they called?
Deliberately unfrozen. The old six-container list (main compartment, archive case, RAVEN, CROWN, WELL, travel wallet) and its codes have been removed. Decide this after the postcard puzzles exist, not before — the containers should be shaped around the puzzles.
Direction, 23 Sept 2026: the release chain in PC-18 needs 13 locks. Use small lockable pouches inside the bag’s pockets rather than one pocket per lock. How many pockets, and which pouches sit in which, is still open (ST-04).
ST-02 Open
How many puzzles sit between the first and the last?
Unknown, and fine to leave unknown. Candidates are in the Options tab.
ST-03 Open
What does the opening four-digit code actually open?
Depends on ST-01.
Per PC-18, 23 Sept 2026: 1021 opens the pouch holding LD and Leif’s map. Which pocket that pouch is in is still open.
ST-04 Needs the bag
Which pockets accept reusable locks without permanent alteration?
Cannot be answered until a backpack is bought. See the candidate in Props & specs.
ST-05 Open
The dig site: a fifth gridded sheet, and what it is for.
Half-settled already: the ticket and her closing letter both say Norway, so the dig is in Norway and Aud’s Iceland map cannot be the sheet that locates it.
Direction proposed: a fifth letter sheet — Liv’s excavation site plan, gridded, living in the last container with the ticket. Real digs are laid out on lettered and numbered squares, so it is the one map in the game whose grid needs no excuse; it keeps the four trail maps visually clean for the shapes drawn over them (PZ-06); and it has metres-per-square resolution where a grid on Harald’s 2,800 km map would be about 200 km per square and locate nothing.
Its job is not a puzzle. The ending is decided as a non-puzzle — “let the reward land” — so this must not become a new gate. Two roles are open to it: pure payoff, and one ungated, optional noticing-reward. Every puzzle in the bag is currently required; nothing rewards attention that is not load-bearing, while the closing line promises exactly that (“someone who notices what everyone else walks past”). An optional find makes that line a payoff rather than a compliment, and costs nothing when missed.
Content idea, not yet decided: mark the squares where she found things, and make the finds the props in the bag — the bone comb, a gaming piece, the three coins, the rune stick. That retroactively explains the whole kit as replicas of her finds. One square left marked but unnamed is the hook for the sleeping-raven ending if that is ever taken.
Open: where in Norway (invent the site rather than borrow a real excavation, or HI-05 applies); whether the optional find is written in plain ink or in UV — plain ink is more findable, which matters more than it sounds for something optional, while UV would finally give the blacklight a job; and whether the site plan is worth a sheet at all if it stays pure payoff.
PC

Postcard system

PC-01 Decided
The four digits of the opening code, and how each luggage tag shows its place.
Code is 1021 — the year tree-ring dating placed the Norse at L’Anse aux Meadows. Each tag is a fictional hotel luggage tag: a printed “If found, please return to” line Liv never filled in properly, with a hand-written hotel name and address instead. The street number is the only number on the tag, so it stands out without needing extra emphasis. Tag A: Vinland Trail Lodge, 10 Skipper’s Wharf, L’Anse aux Meadows, NL. Tag B: Hôtel Rollon, 21 avenue Rollo, Rouen, France.
PC-02 Resolved, 16 Sept 2026
What happens to the underlined 07 already printed on card L1’s postmark?
Resolved by removing postmark dates entirely. Postcards carry no date at all — see PC-03/PC-04. Card L1's postmark keeps the underline's original problem moot: there is no date field left to carry it. Card L1's back needs rebuilding without the 07 JUL mark (not yet done — listed in the priority order at the top of this tab).
PC-03 Removed, 16 Sept 2026
What span do the eighteen dates cover, and do postmarks carry a year?
No longer applicable. Decided, 16 Sept 2026: postmarks carry no date at all, not even as flavour — only the place. Order is entirely reconstructed from ticket dates, postcard statements and the final bundle (PZ-18, Final riddle tab), never from anything printed on a card back. This question no longer has an answer to give.
PC-04 Removed, 16 Sept 2026
The seventeen unassigned postmark dates.
No longer applicable — see PC-03. There is no date to assign on any of the 22 cards, card L1 included; its existing 07 JUL mark is a leftover to strip, not a template to extend.
PC-05 Open
Family D — Châlus, Battle, Ladoga, Anatolia — has no selection rule.
Every other family is selected by something physical or something she wrote. Either invent a rule as good as the others, or redistribute those four cards and drop the family.
PC-06 Hold-to-light decided
What the hold-to-light pair and the comb-grille set actually yield.
Hold-to-light (cards L2/L3) decided: the 1576 the top-edge join produces is lock 3 in Leif's sequence (PZ-13) — it opens a fourth container holding new material, not yet designed. Comb-grille set still has no answer — cards assigned, mechanism decided, nothing wired to it yet.
PC-07 18 of 18 written, 18 of 18 built (updated 19 Sept 2026)
The eighteen Fun Facts.
Real trivia per place, source-checked, with the numbers any code-bearing family needs already living inside them. Written and built, front and back, as of 19 Sept 2026: Leif's L1, L2, L3; Rollo's R1, R2, R3, R4, R5, R6; Aud's A1, A2, A3; Harald's H1, H2, H3, H4, H5; and all four decoys, LD, RD, AD, HD — see PZ-05. H6 (Harald's leg-closing card) is also built, 19 Sept 2026. All 22 cards are complete.
PC-08 Decided
Card L1’s message says L’Anse aux Meadows is the first stop. The route plan made it the third.
Flipped Leif’s trail instead of the sentence: it now runs L’Anse aux Meadows → Markland → Helluland in Norse_Aunt_Route_Plan.json. L’Anse aux Meadows is genuinely first, so card L1’s message is literally true, not just a generous reading of it. Card L1’s postmark carries no date at all now (PC-03/PC-04, removed), so this no longer needs a value — the message and route order already establish it as first.
PC-09 Decided · card L1 done
The image credit is printed on the back of the card itself.
Original objection: a file path on a prop that is meant to be a postcard from a gift shop. The proposed fix was to move attribution to the print-sheet margin outside the trim line, since the credits page plus a sheet-level credit satisfies the licence.
Overtaken in part. The credit now has a second job: it is the camouflage for the publisher’s imprint that carries the F1 grid reference (PZ-10). Players skip that grey strip because it is visibly boring, and the imprint hides inside it. Strip the credit off the card and the imprint becomes the only small grey text on an otherwise clean layout — more conspicuous, not less. Decided: the attribution stays on the card.
The immersion point still stands, though, and it is narrower than it first looked. What breaks the gift-shop illusion is not the credit — real postcards print photographer credits — it is the line Source and full attribution: Postcards/Image_Credits.html. A file path is the only anachronism on the card.
Done on card L1. The Source and full attribution file-path line is dropped; one credit line remains — Image adaptation: “L’Anse aux Meadows, The Meeting of Two Worlds” — D. Gordon E. Robertson, CC BY-SA 3.0. That carries the modification notice, title, author and licence, so it satisfies CC BY-SA 3.0 on its own; the source URL and adaptation licence stay on the credits page. The imprint sits right-aligned opposite it on the same baseline.
Cards L2 and L3 are not done, and must not be until PC-14 is fixed — their printed URLs are currently their only attribution record anywhere.
PC-14 Decided
The credits page now documents all three cards, and 02 and 03 print one credit line each.
Was: Image_Credits.html had one entry, Postcard L1. Cards L2 and L3 carried their attribution only on the cards themselves, as four printed lines each including two full URLs — so the anachronism PC-09 removed from card L1 was still there, and worse, but stripping it would have deleted the project’s only record of those attributions.
Done, in that order. Image_Credits.html gained matching entries for Postcard L2 (“Battle Harbour - MacGillivray”, Matt MacGillivray, CC BY 2.0) and Postcard L3 (“Grinnell Glacier Bergie Bits, Baffin Island”, Gregory “Slobirdr” Smith, CC BY-SA 2.0), each with original work, photographer, source file page, licence, changes and adaptation licence, sourced from References/README.md. Only then were the printed credits reduced to one line each, in card L1’s form.
No URL is now printed on any card back. Note the licence difference the page records: card L2’s source is CC BY 2.0 with no ShareAlike condition, while 01 and 03 are ShareAlike and their adaptations must be licensed alike.
Judgement call worth knowing: the printed line names the source’s licence but not the adaptation’s, and gives no licence URI. For ShareAlike sources that notice now lives only on the credits page, which ships in the repository alongside the PDFs. Reasonable for the medium, and the same choice as card L1 — but it is a choice, not a technicality.
PC-15 9 of 10 built
Which postcards carry Liv’s ten Bayeux-style margin drawings, and in what reading order?
The cross, bow, lightning bolt, tree, teacup, Roman numeral I, arrow/2 hybrid, plain “to” arrow, fish-scale patch and five-pip die are built as a coherent multicolour transparent set and are explicitly Liv’s drawings, not publisher furniture.
Nine now built, per PZ-14: cross + bow + lightning bolt on Rollo’s Bayeux card (stop 3); tree + teacup on his Winchester card (stop 4); fish-scales + Roman numeral I + plain arrow + die-5 on his Battle card (stop 5), reading SCALE 1 TO 5. Bayeux is the card that establishes why the drawings start appearing at all.
Still open: the arrow/2 hybrid, the only icon of the ten with no card assignment and no confirmed puzzle to belong to.
PC-13 Decided · 5 × 3.5 in
The card size is 5 × 3.5 in. All three existing front illustrations now match it; the 02/03 collection build still needs conversion.
Decided: 5 × 3.5 in (360 × 252 pt, 10:7) is the standard for all eighteen cards.
Front artwork done: cards L1–L3 were reframed from 1536 × 1024 to exactly 1500 × 1050 by taking the same exact-ratio crop (38 px from each side, 2 px from the bottom, preserving the top row) and applying one uniform scale. Nothing was stretched. Cards L2 and L3 received the identical centred horizontal crop, so their mirrored cloud-fleck positions stayed in register.
Join re-proofed: the rebuilt 4× seam proof still reads 1576; build_topedge_test_sheet.py now uses 300 dpi and a true 5 in strip, and its PDF was regenerated.
Still to do: build_postcard_collection.py remains at 1536 × 1024 on a 6 × 4 in page, so cards L2 and L3 are not fully converted until its SIZE and PAGE change together and the collection outputs are rebuilt.
Superseded: the earlier state had all three fronts at 3:2 and the join proof at 256 dpi. The choice between resizing 02/03 and enlarging card L1 was resolved in favour of the 5 × 3.5 in standard.
PC-10 To fix
The postmark reads as a logo, not a cancel.
Crisp teal type in a clean double circle, sitting beside the stamp. It should be near-black at 60–70% opacity, rotated 3–8°, with a broken arc, struck across the stamp edge. The styling fix still stands, but the reason has changed: under the PZ-18 candidate direction the postmark carries the place only, not the endgame's ordering key — order is reconstructed from ticket dates, postcard statements and the final bundle instead (see the Final riddle tab).
PC-11 Fronts done · collection pending
Re-render or reframe the three existing fronts at true size.
Artwork complete: all three illustration sources and generated fronts are exactly 1500 × 1050. The guarded front builder and card L1 PDF builder both pass. The deterministic exact-ratio reframe removes the former 5% print stretch without inventing scenery; cards L2/L3 use the same symmetric horizontal crop and the refreshed seam proof reads 1576.
Remaining production step: change SIZE and PAGE together in build_postcard_collection.py, then rebuild and inspect cards L2/L3 and the full collection PDF.
PC-12 Decided
Fun Fact's voice: always typed.
Considered letting it vary per card (typed or handwritten), but that let her handwriting carry two different jobs on the same card — trivia and voice/tasks — with nothing telling them apart, and broke verb D on any card where it happened. Fun Fact is typed on all 18; her handwriting keeps its one job.
PC-16 Decided, 16 Sept 2026
Three different postcard orderings exist and must not be conflated: per-trail order, canonical numbering, and in-game release order.
Decided: no postcards are held back (supersedes PZ-11 — see there and the Postcards tab). All 22 cards, decoys included, are free to hand out through the game.
Decided: within each trail's own sequence, the decoy's slot is chosen for narrative fit — wherever it genuinely fits Aunt Liv's travel story for that leg — not a fixed position (e.g. "always last") and not chosen to optimize the map-shape read. Where exactly that puts each of the four decoys is still open, since it depends on writing each decoy's place in her travel story, which hasn't happened yet.
Canonical numbering is now decided (PC-17) and, by construction, is the same thing as per-trail order — a card's code already states its trail and its position in it. Still open, not yet started: the in-game release/reveal order (PC-18) remains a separate question — do not assume the code scheme settles it.
PC-17 Decided, 16 Sept 2026
The canonical numbering for all 22 cards: trail letter + position in that trail; decoy = D.
Decided. Each card's code is its trail's first letter plus its 1-based position in that trail's real stops; each trail's decoy is that letter plus D, regardless of where the decoy sits narratively (PC-16). This deliberately collapses "canonical numbering" and "per-trail order" into one code — there is no longer a separate continuous 01–22 sequence running across trail blocks.
Codes: Leif — L1 L'Anse aux Meadows, L2 Battle Harbour, L3 Baffin Island, LD Brattahlíð (decoy). Rollo — R1 Châlus, R2 Rouen, R3 Bayeux, R4 Winchester, R5 Battle, R6 Roumare Forest, RD Walcheren (decoy). Aud — A1 Dögurðarnes, A2 Hvammur, A3 Esjuberg, AD Bjarnarhöfn (decoy). Harald — H1 Oslo, H2 Staraya Ladoga, H3 Kyiv, H4 Hedeby, H5 Sicily, H6 Asia Minor, HD Constantinople (decoy).
Done: every built asset (postcard fronts/backs/illustrations/ImageGen sources, print PDFs, build scripts, and this page's own filenames, gallery headings and trail-grid labels) is renamed to the new codes. References/README.md updated to match.
PC-18 Sequence decided, 23 Sept 2026
The in-game release order: which lock or story beat actually puts each of the 22 cards in the player's hands, and when.
Decided in chat, 23 Sept 2026: one linear chain of 13 locks. Every one of the 22 cards is released exactly once, and each step holds what the next puzzle needs. The trails run in order Leif → Rollo → Aud → Harald. Physically, locks are small lockable pouches inside the bag’s pockets, not one pocket per lock (ST-01).

What this settles: the 1576 hold-to-light code (lock 3), which had no destination, now opens Rollo’s first pouch. The architect’s scale ruler arrives with R3–R5, where the rebus first needs it.
Changes from earlier plans: the L’Anse ticket now starts in the open pocket instead of arriving with LD and the map (PZ-13). 2648 now opens H2/H3/HD, and MEAD opens the hnefatafl pouch — the reverse of the 22 Sept plan in PZ-02. The comb is required again, not optional.
Still open: the rebus-distance code (step 6, PZ-14); which AD letters the comb exposes to spell BOOK (step 9); how many four-letter word locks exist for SOLE, BOOK and MEAD (resolved: three owned, PR-27); lock hardware per step overall (roughly 7 four-digit, 3 three-digit, 3 four-letter); and the final bundle’s crest/logo note, assumed to travel with the journal at step 12.
PC-19 Fixed
Six of Harald's fronts (H2–H6, HD) didn't match the series' title-band layout — the title sat directly on the scenery instead of a blank navy band.
Found while syncing this session's new artwork into the page. Every other built front (L1–LD, R1–RD, A1–AD, and Harald's own H1 Oslo) reserves the lower ~20% of the card as a completely blank deep-navy panel, with the place name and country drawn into that empty panel afterward — that's the standard PC-13 fixes for all eighteen cards. H2–H6 and HD were generated full-bleed with the title and subtitle baked directly onto the illustration itself; their own generation prompts said "the title band will be added separately," but that compositing step never ran.
Fixed, same session: build_postcard_front_images.py now paints the missing band itself for exactly these six (a new NEEDS_BAND set, checked by output filename) — a solid deep-navy rectangle over the lower 20%, plus the thin warm-cream divider line sampled from the already-correct cards' own bands, then the existing title/subtitle drawing code runs on top exactly as it does for the other sixteen. Rebuilt from each card's own clean Illustration_v1.png (which never had a baked title), not by patching over the broken _Front.png, so there's no leftover double-text artifact. The sixteen already-correct fronts were not touched — confirmed by hash before/after.
PZ

Puzzles and clues

PZ-01 Decided at 11×11
Hnefatafl: exactly three moves, and the code is the leg lengths.
Board size changed to 11×11 (from 7×7, for realism) and the user designed the final layout themselves in the board designer — 16 attackers, 6 defenders, king on the true centre throne (F6). Exhaustive search over that exact layout confirms exactly three moves has one solution: F6 → H6 → H11 → K11. The legs measure 2, 5 and 3 squares, giving code 253. (Four moves has 18 solutions, same non-uniqueness pattern as the old 7×7 layout, so three stays the right move count.) The old 7×7 solution (231) is fully superseded — not a fallback, not a parallel option.
The hidden-strip mechanism scales cleanly. Columns A–H stay visible on the map back; columns I–K (3 columns, holding 3 attackers plus corners K1/K11) are lost to the stain and recovered on the ticket. Verified by search: assuming those three columns are empty still finds 31 candidate three-move routes, so the puzzle still refuses to resolve rather than resolving wrongly — the same self-checking property the 7×7 version had at 18 candidates.
Rebuilt: Props/Hnefatafl/board_layout.py (new 11×11 geometry, attackers/defenders now tracked separately), build_board_setup_pdf.py and build_ticket_pdf.py (both draw attacker vs. defender as visually distinct tokens now that the layout has both), and the interactive prototype in the Prototype tab (board, blockers, reset/hint routes all updated to match).
Setup still arrives via the map back and the museum ticket (PZ-07), still on Harald's trail (the Oslo postcard, stop 1) — blocked on Harald's map sheet, which does not exist yet. Liv's task line on the Oslo postcard (PZ-05) still reads “exactly three moves,” which is now correct again since the new layout's unique solution also happens to be three moves.
PZ-02 Mechanism approved · perch print pending
HRAFN selects five perches and four flight digits.
H4/H5 and the corrected map still decode HRAFN. Follow H–R–A–F–N on the new flight card: 2, 6, 4, 8. One four-digit lock directly secures H1, the board, pieces, ticket and challenge rules. Release the perch card with H4/H5 after SOLE, outside that pouch. Superseded, 23 Sept 2026 (PC-18): 2648 now opens the pouch with H2, H3 and HD. The hnefatafl pouch opens with MEAD. The perch card arrives with H4/H5 after the comb (BOOK), not directly after SOLE. No cryptex or key. See PR-26 and Approved changes.
Superseded specification · before 22 September 2026
PZ-02 Mechanism decided, 17 Sept 2026
The cryptex answer, and what opening it releases.
HRAFN fits a five-ring cryptex. It was also briefly assigned to the word lock, but that lock is now a physical 4-letter lock (see PZ-03), so HRAFN (5 letters) only fits the cryptex.
Delivery decided: the rune stick and museum label are dropped. The five branch-rune stems live on Harald's own trail map instead, at grid cells G6, A10, I9, C3, F2, among seven meaningless decoy stems elsewhere on the sheet. Postcard H4 carries the decode key (the three rune groups, and that left branches count the group while right branches count the position within it). Postcard H5 carries the reading order: the word RAVEN set apart in its text. Harald's sheet is relettered on its grid band only — A, C, E, I, K, N, R, T, V in place of the shared A–I run — so the five real cells fall under columns spelling RAVEN (R6, A10, V9, E3, N2). Reading them in that literal order gives H, R, A, F, N. Full mechanism and the worked example: Props & specs.
H4 and H5 built, 17 Sept 2026: both cards' messages, Fun Facts and the puzzle content (H4's hand-copied rune key, H5's underlined raven) are done, front and back — see the Postcard system tab.
Still open: which container this feeds (ST-01).
PZ-03 Mechanism approved · print changes pending
Aud’s coin-value tally gives SOLE.
Three piles, one per real-history currency (dirham/denier/Viking), each its own small natural find with a common-to-rare spread. The cache mark (the fehu rune beside the treasure cave, matched to the ticket’s Coin Room key, 24 Sept 2026) selects the Viking pile from the loose hoard; its three coins (rates 5, 100, 3600) convert to 3705, recorded and rotated to read SOLE. A1 and A3 messages rewritten by the user and rebuilt, 25 Sept 2026 (“pretty penny”, “head is spinning”). See Approved changes and the hoard prop for the full currency/coin design. Device choice and physical coin fabrication remain open (PR-24/25).
Superseded specification · before 22 September 2026
PZ-03 Answer decided; card assignment revised 16 Sept 2026
Aud’s coin weight supplies the owned four-letter-lock answer.
Revised 16–17 Sept 2026. The weighing instruction moved from Hvammur to Dögurðarnes, since Hvammur was reassigned to the treasure-map code step (PZ-17). Esjuberg’s half is unchanged. The map code is now the three-digit plot sum 467 (redesigned 17 Sept 2026, superseding 4816); its clue copy and final artwork remain open.
Changed from a five-letter to a four-letter lock: the owned lock takes SOLE; KAMBR (5 letters) is dropped from this slot and MEAD is superseded (now a pinned idea for Harald’s trail instead, PZ-20).
Mechanism: after the treasure-map code opens the hoard, players sort all released coins into their three types. The treasure square’s cache mark identifies the one pile to weigh. Dögurðarnes gives the weighing task; Esjuberg’s upside-down art and Liv’s scale-display sketch, with the final digit crossed out, tell players to read 370.56 as 370.5, then upside down as SOLE.
Still open: exact route clues, coin designs/counts and physical mass calibration (PZ-17, PR-02).

The wording of the three redirect clues.
Clue 1 now falls out of the continuation set (WEIGH WHAT SHE TRADED). Clue 3 is written (EVERY CARD IS A PLACE). Clue 2 depended on KAMBR (the lock opening naming the comb prop); now that PZ-03 proposes MEAD for a four-letter lock instead, clue 2 has no candidate wording again. Stale, 25 Sept 2026: PZ-03’s lock answer is SOLE; MEAD moved to Harald (PZ-20). Clue 1 (“WEIGH WHAT SHE TRADED”) no longer fits either — nothing in the game is weighed any more. Both clues need new wording.
PZ-05 18 of 18 real built (updated 19 Sept 2026) · all 4 decoys built (LD, RD, AD, HD)
The aunt’s letters and card messages.
They carry the reading direction for the rune stick, the comb’s disguise, and every selection and ordering rule. The largest unwritten piece, and where essentially all of the humour lives.
Voice standard decided: enthusiastic, warm, fun, friendly — matching card L1’s register (“Hello, nephew!” … “With love, Aunt Liv”). Every card’s message should read like that, not just card L1’s.
Card L2 (Markland/Battle Harbour) is built. Message: “Second stop: Markland, ‘Forest Land’ in the old sagas, and they weren’t exaggerating, it is gorgeous! Trees for miles, beautiful light and no one around! It sounded like something with claws walked past the cabin last night — I didn’t sleep a wink! Still, you’d love it here. Love, Aunt Liv.” Fun Fact: the saga-landfall/Labrador identification (no date needed, so nothing pending on PC-03/PC-04 had to be guessed). Built in build_postcard_L2_pdf.py, following card L1’s exact standard layout (PC-13) — see PC-07.
The animal mention (“something with claws”) is deliberately just flavour. Superseded, 15 Sept 2026: this card no longer carries any imprint — the real Vinland Editions · Series F, No. 1 beasts-chain pointer moved to the new decoy card LD (PZ-10/PZ-18). Postmark carries the place only, no date — the eighteen dates are still unassigned (PC-03/PC-04); inventing one would risk conflicting with PZ-08’s trail-order constraint once real dates exist.
Card L3 (Helluland/Baffin Island) is also built. Message: “New stop: Helluland, ‘Flat Stone Land’ to the sagas — and they weren’t kidding about the stone either, not a tree in sight! But last night the sky put on a show I’ll never forget: green and purple curtains rippling right overhead. I stood out in the cold far too long just watching. Almost missed my supper! Love, Aunt Liv.” Fun Fact: the saga/Baffin Island identification (parallels card L2’s approach rather than repeating the aurora content already in the message). Built in build_postcard_L3_pdf.py. Superseded, 15 Sept 2026: its flavour imprint (Series C, No. 3) has been removed — cards L1–L3 no longer carry any imprint (PZ-18). Revised, 19 Sept 2026: the opener changed from “Third stop” to “New stop” so the card no longer numbers itself, and the matching pos clue was dropped from the solver. Answer-neutral — L3 is still third by elimination — but it makes the crest filter necessary to tell L3 from decoy LD. Rationale and the warning against doing the same to L2 are in the Final riddle tab (PZ-18).
Historical bug, no longer applicable: while building card L3, card L1’s imprint was caught still reading Series F, No. 1 — the actual pointer — left over from before PZ-13 moved it to card L2. Two cards briefly claimed to be the same answer. Fixed to a flavour value (Series A, No. 1) at the time, and since removed entirely.
Card LD (Brattahlíð, Leif's decoy) is built, front and back. Message: “Amazing place today! Brattahlid, Greenland where Leif actually grew up. It's stunning here, green against the ice in a way the name ‘Greenland’ never prepared me for. Erik apparently picked that name on purpose, to lure settlers! I really enjoy those small details! Found the foundations of the little church his wife had built, right on the fjord. With Love, Aunt Liv.” The handwriting spells it “Brattahlid” without the eth on purpose — the handwriting font renders ð as a broken glyph, confirmed against the typed Fun Fact box, which renders Brattahlíð and Þjóðhildar's correctly. Fun Fact: Erik the Red's homestead near modern Qassiarsuk seeded Greenland's Eastern Settlement, and its ruins include Þjóðhildar's Church, a small turf chapel built around the year 1000. Carries the real Vinland Editions · Series F, No. 1 imprint (PZ-10/PZ-18) — this is also the first card to actually carry the “You know me — every little detail counts” rule line (PZ-10), worked into the message itself (“I really enjoy those small details!”) rather than printed separately. Built in build_postcard_LD_pdf.py; front art by Codex, sourced from a CC BY 2.0 Wikimedia photograph of the reconstructed church (see the Postcards tab for the credit and prompt).
All four of Leif’s cards (three real, one decoy) are now fully built, front and back, in their own dedicated scripts (build_postcard_{L1,L2,L3,LD}_pdf.py); the shared build_postcard_collection.py still only assembles cards L1–L3 (its own CARDS list is deliberately left empty for 04 onward, per its own comment) and has no cards of its own left in placeholder state.
Rollo’s trail now carries an explicit family-line thread (Rollo → William the Conqueror → Richard the Lionheart), per the “more history” direction — each built card names the real history and the descent, not just scenery.
Bayeux (stop 3) is fully built, front and back. Message: “Today was Bayeux and its famous Norman tapestry. The tapestry was awesome! It tells the story of William the Conqueror taking England in 1066 — and get this, he’s one of Rollo’s own descendants! I’m obsessed with the style and the details, I spent hours looking at it. You know how much I like drawing (despite being terrible at it), so don’t be surprised if you see a few drawings here and there. Love, Aunt Liv.” This is the card that explains, in-fiction, why small Bayeux-style drawings start turning up in her later cards — and it carries the first one itself: the cross/bow/lightning-bolt rebus (CROSSBOW BOLT → Châlus) is scattered in the blank space below her signature (PZ-14). Fun Fact: “The Bayeux Tapestry shows Halley’s Comet blazing over King Harold’s court just before Hastings — the earliest known depiction of the comet, centuries before it got its name. A Victorian replica by the Leek Embroidery Society later added underpants to some of the tapestry’s more, ah, undressed figures.” Built in build_postcard_R3_pdf.py.
Winchester (stop 4) is fully built, front and back. Message: “Winchester next — freezing here, but worth every minute. This cathedral is where Richard the Lionheart was crowned a second time, in 1194, after finally being released from years held captive abroad. Another one of Rollo’s own line! Funny to think it all traces back to one deal, centuries earlier. Love, Aunt Liv.” Names Richard I explicitly and loops toward the treaty without naming Saint-Clair-sur-Epte, so the rebus still does its own job. Carries the tree/teacup rebus (TREATY → Saint-Clair-sur-Epte) below the signature — the second piece of the chain Bayeux started (PZ-14, revised from an earlier draft that put the crossbow-bolt rebus here instead). Fun Fact: the genuine (non-Arthurian) medieval Round Table in Winchester's Great Hall, tree-ring dated to c.1290, repainted for Henry VIII. Both real, verifiable, no date-conflict — separate from the 1194 recoronation year already in the message. Built in build_postcard_R4_pdf.py; Rollo’s other four cards have no dedicated scripts yet.
Battle (stop 5) is fully built, front and back. Message: “Today I visited Battle, the village where the Battle of Hastings happened. This is where William the Conqueror fought King Harold. William’s victory sealed the conquest of England by the Normans. It’s so cool to be able to see it in person, especially after spending hours looking at the tapestry! Love, Aunt Liv.” Names the 1066 victory explicitly and loops back to the Bayeux Tapestry from card R3. Carries a third rebus below the signature — fish-scales + Roman numeral I + plain arrow + die-5, reading SCALE 1 TO 5, naming which of the architect’s ruler scales to use for the Châlus–Saint-Clair-sur-Epte distance (corrected 25 Sept 2026 from “Bayeux–Winchester”: the rebuses on those cards point to Châlus and Saint-Clair, and the map icons measured are there) (PZ-14). Fun Fact: “The Bayeux Tapestry’s famous scene of Harold clutching an arrow in his eye may not be original — Victorian restorers reworked stitching in that exact spot, so historians still argue about how his death was actually shown.” Also loops back to card R3. Built in build_postcard_R5_pdf.py; Rollo’s other three cards (Châlus, Rouen, Roumare) have no dedicated scripts yet.
Rouen (stop 2, postcard R2) message and Fun Fact are decided; front artwork is built, while the back and print PDF remain to build. Message: “Today I got to visit Rouen, and it might be my favourite so far!
This was Rollo’s own capital! The King gave Rollo all of Normandy so the fighting would stop.
Can’t walk anywhere without counting my steps — always have. This afternoon was the city proper, the cathedral and the famous half-timbered houses. The city was vibrant today with lots of people milling about.
All that walking around made me crave fast-food: the battered poultry from Kentucky. Tonight’s dinner was a bit fancier, the Inn I’m staying at is famous for their snails!
With love,
Aunt Liv” Revised, 19 Sept 2026: the forest/cow sentence is cut (PZ-15 dropped from a six-word to a four-word chain) and replaced with a rule-line in the same family as LD’s “every little detail counts” — this one plants the squares-counting readout instead of the old direction readout. Fun Fact, both supplied facts combined into one callout: “Rouen Cathedral holds the actual heart of Richard the Lionheart, King of England and Duke of Normandy. Its spire also once held a record of its own: at 151 metres (495 feet) it’s the tallest church spire in France, and was briefly the tallest structure in the world in the late 19th century.”
Carried the word-lock order clue (PZ-15), original six-word order. By first mention: fight (“the fighting would stop”), forest, cow (“Normande cows”), people, poultry, inn. Superseded, 19 Sept 2026: the forest/cow sentence is cut, dropping those two pairs. Surviving order: fight, people, poultry, inn — “fighting” for fight stays an inflected form, kept deliberately as a small extra matching step.
Resolved against each pair's fixed ticket coordinate (PZ-15): fight → I6, people/folk → F7, poultry/hen → A3, inn → B9. The lock is now a 4-digit numeric combo, not a directional lock — see PZ-15 for the squares-counting readout and the code 1486. Directional readout (RIGHT, DOWN, DOWN, UP, LEFT, LEFT) and the six-word order are kept below as the record of the superseded mechanism.
Supersedes both earlier order drafts (forest/combat/tavern/people/beef/poultry, then forest/folk/fight/tavern/beef/poultry) sketched before this final text existed.
Built, front and back. Front illustration adapted from a real Place du Vieux-Marché photograph (credited on the card back; full source in Postcards/References/README.md). No rebus on this card. Built in build_postcard_R2_pdf.py. Superseded, 15 Sept 2026: its flavour imprint (Series B, No. 5) has been removed (PZ-18).
Hvammur (stop 2, postcard A2) — front, back and print PDF all built, 19 Sept 2026. Message rewritten from its original 16 Sept 2026 draft to resolve the treasure-hunt's final leg (PZ-17). Message: “Hvammur today.
Such a lovely place and a great museum dedicated to Aud.
They even had a treasure hunt to find artifacts from Aud’s time.
I was able to solve it and had a lovely time discovering the countryside. I particularly enjoyed the road that followed the forest.
I might just have to remember how many rivers bridges, fords and gates I crossed. (“rivers” changed to “bridges”, 25 Sept 2026, to match the ticket’s columns)
With love,
Aunt Liv” Fun Fact: “Aud claimed the entire valley between two rivers here in Dalasýsla, then freed every thrall aboard her ships and granted each one land of their own. One, Vífill, got the valley still called Vífilsdalur today. She’s one of very few women named as a primary land-claimant in Landnámabók, Iceland’s own settlement record.” Sourced via Wikipedia and Guide to Iceland. Complete, 16 Sept 2026. The message now carries the treasure hunt's resolving clue — superseded twice since: first to name which branch to take at the standing stone, now to point at the correct village-at-a-crossing (H9) over its lookalike near H3/G3 — see PZ-17 for the full mechanism and the current code, 521. Back and print PDF are the only pieces left to build.
Harald’s leg opens with H4 and H5, both fully built, front and back, 17 Sept 2026 — the branch-rune cipher (PZ-02). H4 (Hedeby) message: “New stop: Hedeby, though there's barely a Hedeby left to stand in — just a green ring of earthworks where the ramparts used to run. A woodcarver at the reconstruction huts showed me how the old branch-runes worked — count the branches one way for the family, the other way for the letter. I copied his key out for you before I forgot it. Love, Aunt Liv,” followed by her hand-drawn copy of the 3-group rune table. Fun Fact: Harald burning Hedeby to the ground in 1050, sailing burning ships into the harbour — archaeologically corroborated by charred timbers found in the Schlei. Built in build_postcard_H4_pdf.py. H5 (Aci Castello, Sicily) message: “Aci Castello today. A Norman Castle in Sicily! It's crazy to think how far they travelled! Being a good tourist, I enjoyed a gelato while walking around the ruins. Little did I know, that is a great way to attract wildlife. A raven followed me the whole time! Half convinced it was you, checking up on me. Love, Aunt Liv.” The underlined raven is the reading-order clue. Fun Fact, deliberately a different fact from the message’s own Norman-castle mention: “Aci Castello's rock is over 500,000 years old — lava that cooled underwater into a mass of glassy basalt pillars. The castle itself later spent three centuries as a prison, until an 1818 earthquake made it too unsafe to hold anyone.” Built in build_postcard_H5_pdf.py. H5's front art depicts Aci Castello, not Syracuse (the site named in stops.js for Harald’s real 1038–1040 Sicilian campaign) — the text was retargeted to match the existing art rather than the art redone.
Oslo (H1) is next in Harald’s leg, fully built front and back, 17 Sept 2026. Message: “I'm spending a whole week in Oslo, so much to do! It's Harald's city and has so much Viking history. Found the quirkiest little museum near the harbour, all about hnefatafl, the chess-like old board game the Vikings loved and that we used to play with our own rules when you were younger. Grabbed you a little souvenir from the gift shop so you can try — can you get the king to escape in exactly 3 moves? Count how far he travels each time. Love, Aunt Liv.” Supersedes the earlier “Last stop” draft, written when Oslo was conceived as ending Harald’s trail rather than opening it. Fun Fact: “Snorri's saga credits Harald with founding Oslo around 1050 — but a church and cemetery already stood there by the year 1000, and in 2026 archaeologists found a Viking boat grave buried beneath the site of his own royal manor, older than his reign.” This is where Liv’s task line for the hnefatafl puzzle lives (PZ-01) — the museum ticket itself only explains the real historical game, not this puzzle’s house rules. Built in build_postcard_H1_pdf.py; front art already existed (no photo credit recorded for it, unlike H4/H5). No longer blocked on Harald’s trail map — the sheet now exists (PZ-09).
H6 will be the leg’s final-reveal card — decided, not yet written: “This is the last stop of my travel for now. Solve this final puzzle and meet me for more adventure during your university summer break,” with receipts/evidence for the final full puzzle rather than a lock of its own.
H2, H3 and HD carry Harald’s third lock, built 17 Sept 2026 (PZ-20): a MEAD riddle, one attribute word per card via three different riddle techniques. HD stops being a pure decoy for this purpose. H3 (Kyiv) message: “Kyiv next, and it might be the most beautiful city on this whole trip — golden domes catching the light on every hill. Stopped by St. Michael’s this morning and lit a candle for you. Cheesy, I know. Here’s a little riddle for you, the kind we used to trade back and forth: I’m sweet, but no fruit tree grew me. I’m gold, but no smith ever cast me. Thousands of tiny workers made me and set six-sided walls to guard me. Love, Aunt Liv.” Fun Fact, deliberately not honey/bees since the riddle itself is that content: Oleg of Novgorod made Kyiv the Rus’ capital and, per the Primary Chronicle, sailed on Constantinople in 907 — supposedly nailing his shield to the gates (a legend modern historians doubt); the real treaty that followed in 911 gave Rus’ merchants tax-free trading rights there for up to six months. H2 (Staraya Ladoga) message, an acrostic spelling DRINK: “Days here move slower than I expected, and I love it. Rurik supposedly ruled from right here, if the old chronicles are true. I climbed up on the fortress wall for a look at the whole river bend. Nobody warned me how far this feels from anywhere else. Keeping my postcards dry has become a full-time job in this weather.” Fun Fact: the 52-rune Younger Futhark stick found at Staraya Ladoga in 1950 (c. 840–865 AD), one of the oldest runic finds in Russia, now in the Hermitage. HD (Constantinople) message: “Constantinople — well, Istanbul now, but I like the old name for this trip. Impossible not to think about Harald everywhere here; this is where he made his fortune, long before Norway ever heard of him. Found this in a little shop near the Hippodrome, couldn’t resist:” followed by a drawn rebus (sugar cubes + a bubbling jar + an hourglass) reading FERMENTED. Fun Fact, deliberately more specific than the message’s own tease: Harald served in the Byzantine emperor’s Varangian Guard, 1034–1043 under three emperors, and the wealth he brought home funded his claim to Norway’s throne. Full mechanism and solve in the mead entry, Puzzles & locks.
Calendar clauses added and rebuilt, 17 Sept 2026 (PZ-18). Five built cards each gained one sentence so the final riddle’s order can be reconstructed from the cards themselves rather than from date arithmetic. As printed: R1 Châlus — “Odd place to start his family’s story, at the very end of it, but this is where I began.” R2 Rouen — “The tapestry towns are still ahead of me — saving those for later.” Placed after all six Saxon words so the word-lock order (PZ-15) is untouched, and it uses none of them. R6 Roumare — “The last of Rollo’s places on my list, and I’ve ended up right back beside Rouen to finish.” H1 Oslo — second sentence now reads “It’s Harald’s city and the obvious place to begin with him — so much Viking history.” H4 Hedeby — opening now reads “New stop: Hedeby — saltwater again, after all that river country. Barely a Hedeby left to stand in: a green ring of earthworks where the ramparts ran.” The original wording was tightened by a few words at the same time: H4 draws the branch-rune key table below the signature and that box clamps at y = 30, so an extra line of message pushed its caption outside the frame. Ten lines is H4’s ceiling. R2 is the tightest card in the deck at about one spare line. All five rebuilt front and back, with their gallery PNGs refreshed.
Updated, 18 Sept 2026: A1 (Dögurðarnes) and A3 (Esjuberg) are now built, front and back, opening and closing Aud’s trail and carrying the hoard’s weighing and upside-down-reading instructions (PZ-03). Superseded, 22 Sept 2026: weighing is replaced by the coin tally; A1 needs its puzzle paragraph replaced and A3 a small wording update before reprinting (drafts in Approved changes). AD (Bjarnarhöfn) is also built, front and back. Aud’s museum ticket is built (Props/AudTicket/build_aud_ticket_pdf.py). H2, H3 and HD are built (PZ-20’s MEAD riddle). Nothing open here. H6, Harald’s leg-closing card, is built 19 Sept 2026 (PC-07). A2’s back and print PDF is also built, 19 Sept 2026, once the treasure-hunt redesign (PZ-17) landed.
PZ-06 Decided
The final map mechanic: laminated letter prints, drawn on with wet-erase marker.
Four letter sheets, printed and laminated at home. Players sort the deck by stamp, lay each trail’s cards out beside its map in date order, and draw the legs straight onto the laminate. No pins, no cord, no overlay. Wipe to reset.
Pinned cards is eliminated on geometry, not preference. Rouen and Roumare are 10 km apart on a trail spanning 624 km, so they land 0.13 in apart on a letter sheet — and still only 0.38 in apart on a 24 × 36 poster. Two 5-inch postcards cannot occupy that gap at any printable size. Separately, six cards need 105 in² against a letter sheet’s 84 in² of printable area.
Footprint is fine: four letter sheets laid 2 × 2 come to about 17 × 22 in, roughly 1.4 × 1.8 ft, leaving a normal table clear for the cards and props. Larger shop-printed and laminated maps (tabloid at about 1.8 × 2.8 ft for the set) stay available if the drawing ever feels cramped, but they end free same-day reprints.
Specs: gloss pouches, since matte takes marker unevenly, and wet-erase rather than dry-erase — dry-erase smears under a palm resting on a sheet being drawn across, and ghosts if left for days. A laminating pouch also covers both faces at once, which is what PZ-07 needs for the map whose back carries the board setup. 5 mil pouches over 3 mil for a sheet that is drawn on and wiped repeatedly.
Per-trail colour, on the grid band only. Each sheet's border band carries its trail's colour — Leif #216580, Rollo #A2562D, Aud #6D528B, and #467444 for Harald once that sheet exists — with the grid references reversed to white. Four laminated sheets are hard to tell apart on a crowded table, and the band is the only element visible from any edge that carries no map content. Built into build_trail_maps_pdf.py as a band key per trail.
Print on white or natural cardstock, not coloured. The sheet is a full-coverage two-tone illustration (sea #DCE7E4, land #EFE3C4) and printers cannot lay down white, so coloured stock would shift both fills toward the paper and compress a contrast that is already narrow. That contrast is load-bearing rather than decorative: it is what identifies the unlabelled animals on Leif's chart, so it cannot be spent on route identity. Coloured stock is better spent on the paper props — her letters, the museum tickets and their indexes, the luggage tags — where ink coverage is light and the paper colour becomes the design instead of fighting it.
Still untested: that the drawn shapes read as 1972 in a real play-test. Harald’s 2 is the loosest of the four — now that his real sheet is built (PZ-09), the traced route reads more as a zigzag than a digit at all. Accepted anyway: by then one lock remains and the code parses as a year, which self-checks.
PZ-09 Principle set, one open
Every map earns at least one job besides the final puzzle.
Decided as a rule. Four sheets that only matter at the very end announce themselves as endgame props the moment they appear. Each map must do something earlier, so it reads as reference material players happen to be carrying.
Leif — one job, decided. The front carries seven unlabelled animal vignettes whose names are the key to a lookup on the L'Anse museum ticket — see the Beasts on the chart puzzle. The hnefatafl back-of-sheet job moved off Leif's map — see Harald, below.
Aud — decided, and much more than "nearly free." Superseded, 16 Sept 2026: this bullet used to point at the old, dropped KAMBR/Kambsnes idea. The map's actual job is now the full treasure-hunt route (PZ-17) — a seven-stop clue chain across a surveyed Dalir/Hvammur valley, one hop deliberately ambiguous and resolved by a detail in card A2’s message. The sheet turns landscape and splits into two panels: a new detail panel on the left, the existing full-region map on the right, unchanged in content and actually larger than before (scale 0.760 against the old 0.67).
Rollo — candidate. Saint-Clair-sur-Epte is on his sheet, where the 911 grant of Normandy was made. A date or fact beat could hang off it (HI-05 applies). A boar vignette belongs on this sheet — wild boar are native to continental Europe and about as Norse a beast as exists, and Roumare was a hunting forest, so a boar in Roumare is the most natural thing on the map. It was excluded from Leif’s chart on the native-species test.
Harald — three jobs. Decided, 17 Sept 2026: the front now carries the branch-rune cipher (PZ-02) — five carved rune-stems at grid cells G6, A10, I9, C3, F2, among seven decoys, readable once the sheet's relettered grid band (A, C, E, I, K, N, R, T, V) spells out RAVEN. This replaces the earlier unplaced coin/mint-city concept. The back now carries the hnefatafl board setup (PZ-07, reassigned from Leif) — composited, 19 Sept 2026: build_trail_maps_pdf.py now draws the board-setup panel as page 2 of Harald's sheet (reusing build_board_setup_pdf.py's own drawing code, centred on the same letter page and paper tone as the front), so the two print and laminate as one physical object rather than two loose pieces of paper the user has to align by hand.
Watch: a second job on the front is stronger than one on the back, because it uses the geography rather than just the paper. Harald's now has both — the rune cipher on the front, the hnefatafl setup on the back.
Sheet built. Harald's map (Trail_Map_4_Harald_{Print,ANSWER}.pdf) is no longer blocked — his two regional stops (Sicily, Asia Minor) are resolved to named, sourced settlements: Syracuse (captured in the 1038–1040 Byzantine Sicilian campaign Harald served in) and Patara (where a real ninth/tenth-century Viking sword was found in 2018, chosen as an explicitly illustrative representative point — no single city is attested as Harald's own site in Asia Minor). First build succeeded cleanly: all six stops labelled, no collisions. Open, accepted as a known risk: the traced route reads as a zigzag rather than a clean digit 2 — flagged to the user, who chose to accept it as-is rather than reorder the visit sequence (which would also require re-checking PZ-08's postmark-order constraint and PZ-11's held-back-card choice). Title THE VARANGIAN ROAD is a draft pick, not yet confirmed. Decorative regions/areas/waters/extra-town labels (present on all three other sheets) are not yet added — this sheet currently has only the six real stops labelled.
PZ-08 Decided and verified, 17 Sept 2026
The digit order is each trail’s first appearance — read from the reconstructed travel order, not from postmark dates.
The order in which each trail first appears is the order its digit is read. That part still holds.
What changed (PZ-18): the source of that order is no longer a straight sort of postmark dates. Postmarks now carry place only. Order comes instead from reconstructing Liv's calendar — ticket dates, postcard statements and the final bundle — described in the Final riddle tab.
Constraint this places on the still-open calendar. The earliest real (non-decoy) card of each trail must fall in the order Leif → Rollo → Aud → Harald, or the code is not 1972. Postcard L1 (L’Anse aux Meadows) is still the first card of the game, so Leif leading is already true. Decided, 16 Sept 2026: postmarks carry no date at all (PC-02/PC-03/PC-04, removed) — L1's own place in the calendar comes from the reconstructed evidence in the Final riddle tab, not from anything printed on the card.
Checked, 17 Sept 2026. The clue set resolves this uniquely. First appearances land at positions 1, 4, 6 and 8 — Leif, Rollo, Aud, Harald — giving 1972. Verified by NorseBackpack/Tools/final_riddle_clues.py; the ordering is now carried by the postcards’ own text rather than by date arithmetic, since variable stay lengths mean a date is no longer a position.
PZ-07 Rebuilt at 11×11 · map sheet still missing
The board setup, split across the map back and the museum ticket.
Reassigned from Leif to Harald. Superseding the earlier “it is Leif's map” decision below: the user moved this to Harald's trail, arriving with the Oslo postcard (stop 1, PZ-05). Liv wrote the 22 fixed-piece positions (16 attackers, 6 defenders) on the back of the Oslo trail map; the ticket was set down on wet glue and lifted the right-hand strip (columns I–K), leaving an illegible stain on the map and a mirrored copy (columns read K J I left to right — reversed from the map's own I, J, K order) on the ticket's blank back panel. Hold the ticket to the light, align on the grid lines, the board completes.
Rebuilt at 11×11 (PZ-01), both panels, sharing one geometry source so they can't drift apart:
  • Props/Hnefatafl/board_layout.py — the 22 attacker/defender squares, throne and corners, one place, matching the interactive prototype in this page and the user's own board designer export exactly.
  • Props/Hnefatafl/build_board_setup_pdf.py — the map-back panel: columns A–H plus the throne and two visible corners, attackers and defenders drawn as visually distinct tokens (dark filled vs. light outlined squares), with an irregular stain swallowing columns I–K (their squares are never drawn under the stain, not merely covered by it — the earlier 7×7 draft made that mistake and the tokens showed through). Liv's own margin note sits beside the stain (“Oops — ticket was still wet with glue when I set it down here. Sorry, past me. — L”). Output: output/pdf/Hnefatafl_Board_Setup_Insert.pdf — a standalone insert, now composited onto Harald's trail map back (see the map gallery in this tab).
  • Props/Hnefatafl/build_ticket_pdf.py — the ticket's back panel: the same three columns, reversed and matched to the same 0.4in cell pitch, so the grid lines genuinely continue when the two sheets are butted together. The ticket grew from 2.4 × 5.6in to 2.6 × 6.6in to fit the taller three-column, eleven-row panel.
Settled: the grid lines cross the join so registration is self-verifying; the stain leaves the gap visible rather than implied; both marks are printed rather than real ink so the prop resets. New candidate · recorded 2026-09-15: 3D-print the attacker and defender pieces with controlled, concealed weights and a letter or rune on each base.
