The scale must be replaced: the current linked 200 g / 0.01 g model is insufficient. The replacement needs capacity above 370.56 g and 0.01 g resolution. Exact coin counts, designs and controlled ballast remain open until the physical scale and coin blanks are chosen and tested.

Prop

The comb grille

A printed or 3D-printed comb with broken teeth, laid across a postcard message so that only certain words show through the gaps. Rotated or flipped, one comb decodes several cards. Back on Aud’s trail, 16 Sept 2026 — it had briefly moved to Harald’s journey in an earlier session, but the user moved it back: Aud’s own stamp motif and HI-02 already tie a comb to her documented saga story (Landnámabók’s account of the comb she lost at Kambsnes), which fits better than an unconnected Harald assignment ever did.

Combs are among the most common Viking finds and broken teeth are entirely plausible, so the object hides in plain sight. Superseded, 18 Sept 2026: the comb is no longer a family heirloom Liv carries in; it's Aud's own comb, found on the trip and kept — see AD's message below.

Job, decided 16 Sept 2026: laid across decoy card AD’s (Bjarnarhöfn) message, released at the end of Aud’s treasure-hoard chain (PZ-17). The words it exposes open a lock gating entry to Harald’s leg — Aud’s whole trail must be finished before Harald’s can start. The comb will be 3D-printed, which means tooth spacing can be engineered to expose chosen letters exactly rather than relying on a found comb — the grille can be cut to fit AD's message text below rather than the other way around. Candidate word, 18 Sept 2026: BOOK, confirming the reading/kinship idea in AD's message rather than naming it outright. It opens the same owned four-letter combination lock as PZ-03’s SOLE, reset to a new combination for this job.

AD's message, decided 18 Sept 2026:

Superseded, 25 Sept 2026: the first line below was reworded on 20 Sept 2026 to “At her brother’s harbor — the one Aud wintered in.” The built card (build_postcard_AD_pdf.py) uses the new line.

At her brother’s harbor — the one Aud wintered in.
I’ve read everything I could find about her this trip, to the point that she feels like family (and she actually maybe was).
It was so awesome to find that comb alongside the coins, and the museum let me keep it!
This is actually the real treasure, a piece of history that we can use!
With love,
Aunt Liv

Built: Postcards/build_postcard_AD_pdf.py → output/pdf/Postcard_AD_Bjarnarhofn_Print.pdf. See Postcards · AD for the rendered card.

Still open: which specific letters in this text the comb's teeth expose to spell BOOK, and the physical tooth-cutting itself. Resolved 25 Sept 2026 — see below.

Mechanism decided and comb modelled, 25 Sept 2026. The comb lies upright on AD’s back: handle in the left margin (overhanging the card edge by about 5.5 mm), teeth pointing right across the message. One tooth per line, at the message’s own line spacing, so each intact tooth covers its whole line. Four teeth are broken short and stop just before a chosen letter, so with the comb in place only four lines show:
LineWhat showsBroken tooth
1brother’s harbor — the one Aud10.5 mm
3ould find about (the o of “could”)29.5 mm
8ongside the coins, and the museum let (“al” covered)3.1 mm
9keep it!5.2 mm
First visible letters, top to bottom: BOOK. An upright comb was the user’s call: it puts the broken teeth in reading order. A tilted comb was tried first and rejected — its spine and alignment marks would have fallen off the card.

Alignment. Two Viking ring-and-dot marks are printed on AD’s back in the left margin, one above line 1 and one below the signature, 49 mm apart (COMB_MARKS in build_postcard_AD_pdf.py). The comb’s flared ends carry matching carved ring-and-dots whose centres are 2 mm through-holes; players sit each printed dot in its hole. AD’s own stamp shows the same comb with a ring-and-dot at each end, so the stamp quietly shows how the comb goes on.

Handle carving, after the stamp: flared ends with ring-and-dots, a knotwork band along the middle, a small dot at each end of the band, and a border line, all cut 0.6 mm into the top face (lines at least 0.6 mm wide for the 0.4 mm nozzle). The knotwork is currently a placeholder two-strand twist; the real band comes from Codex (prompt below) as Props/Comb/knotwork.svg, and the build uses it automatically once the file exists. Finish suggestion: print in bone-white and rub dark wax or paint into the grooves.

Sizes: about 65 × 55 mm overall; teeth 2.2 mm wide, 1.6 mm thick, intact teeth 52.7 mm long, gaps 0.85 mm; handle 3.0 mm thick, outer corners rounded to 2.5 mm (the carved border follows them); the inside corners where the flared ends meet the handle get only a 0.3 mm fillet, because anything larger closes the gap to the first tooth below 0.75 mm. Flat bottom, printed face up, no supports.

Built: Props/Comb/build_comb.py reads the line positions and target letters straight from the built AD print PDF (so the comb cannot drift from the card), checks the four letters still spell BOOK, and writes Aud_Comb.scad, Aud_Comb.stl, comb_geometry.json and Aud_Comb_on_AD.png (the comb drawn over the real card). Re-run it after any change to AD’s back.

Still open: a test print against a real printed AD (fit, tooth strength, whether the marks survive trimming); the Codex knotwork band.
Exact Codex prompt — knotwork band for the comb handle
Use case: flat relief artwork for 3D printing, to be traced into a vector.
Reference: NorseBackpack/Postcards/Stamps/Stamp_Aud_Comb_v1.png - match the knotwork band on that comb's handle.
Create one horizontal Viking-age ribbon interlace band, as carved on a bone comb handle: two ribbons
weaving over and under each other along the band, with a small curled terminal at each end.
Pure black shapes on a pure white background. No grey, shading, texture, outline border, text or
frame. Show every under-crossing with a clear white gap either side of the ribbon passing under.
Proportions: exactly 8:1 (it will be printed about 38 x 4.8 mm). Every ribbon at least 1/8 of the
band height wide, and every white gap at least as wide, so no line is thinner than 0.6 mm at print size.
Output: PNG, 3200 x 400 px.
Then: threshold at luminance 128 and trace to closed polygons as for the coin faces
(Props/Coins/build_coin_art.py), save as Props/Comb/knotwork.svg, and re-run build_comb.py.
Tools

The three decoding tools and what each is for

Each tool opens a class of clue. Each should have one primary job so it never feels decorative.

UV pen and blacklight

Primary job: the trail reading order. The order in which the four trails are read is currently "left to right," which is arbitrary — the strongest moment in the game resting on the weakest rule. Instead, write four small numerals beside the trails on the map in UV ink. The order becomes something she left for you, and the code 1972 survives untouched.

This is the highest-value use of the pen. Spend it here before anywhere else.

Red acetate filter

Primary job: the illustrations. A red-noise wash over each postcard image; under the filter, one object per card is circled or a latitude appears in the sky. Adds a clue layer with no extra prop.

Needs work Home printers vary a great deal on this. Test colour accuracy before committing any puzzle to it.

Magnifier

Primary job: the branch runes. Carve them small enough that the tally strokes are ambiguous to the naked eye. The magnifier turns "I think that's three branches" into certainty, which makes it a real tool rather than a gimmick.

Mechanic

Three non-lock redirect clues

Puzzles whose answer is an instruction rather than a code. They work best exactly where a lock would be clumsy. Three is the cap — more and the locks stop feeling like the spine.

Rules that keep them from stalling a group

  • Name a prop that is unique. There is one comb, one balance, one board. Never "the card."
  • Name a physical action, not a concept. Sort, hold to the light, fit into, rotate, stack. Never "consider" or "compare."
  • The payoff must be visible within seconds, or players will not believe they did it right.
#RolePlacementPurpose
1TeachThe payoff of the continuation set — four postcards butted together spell out WEIGH WHAT SHE TRADED.Breaks the assumption that every answer is a number, and does it without needing its own puzzle. Self-verifying: the sentence either reads or it does not.
2ToolOpen again — was the answer to a five-letter lock, KAMBR (Old Norse for comb). That lock is now a four-letter lock and takes SOLE instead, for Aud's trail (PZ-03), so this reveal has no mechanism again. MEAD — once proposed for this same slot — is now a pinned idea for a possible second lock on Harald's trail instead (PZ-20).The honest way to reveal that the comb is an instrument rather than a keepsake, without spending a hint — still the goal, just no longer wired to a lock answer.
3HingeAt the map reveal. EVERY CARD IS A PLACE.The pivot of the whole game. A lock would say "you were right." A sentence says "she knew you would get here."
Prop kit · folded in from the Props & choices tab, 17 Sept 2026

Make the bag feel like it belongs to her

Core reusable prop kit

  • The L’Anse aux Meadows museum ticket — fictional Viking Museum of L’Anse aux Meadows, decided (PZ-12): ordinary admission ticket front, A–Z visitor index on the back that keys the beasts-on-the-chart lookup.
  • The raven’s flights card and one four-digit lock set to 2648 directly on the board pouch. HRAFN is the decoded five-perch itinerary. Cryptex and extra keyed closure are superseded (PZ-02, PR-26).
  • An 11 × 11 printed hnefatafl board with 23 pieces: 16 dark, 6 light and a distinctive king. The user can 3D-print pieces. Supply the fixed-piece three-move challenge rules with H1; no captures or jumps.
  • An admission ticket from the (invented) Hnefatafl Museum, Oslo — arrives with the Oslo postcard on Harald's trail. Explains the real historical game (light defenders, dark attackers, sandwich-capture), not this puzzle's own house rules. Its back panel also carries a mirrored offset holding the right-hand strip of the board setup. Built: Props/Hnefatafl/build_ticket_pdf.py.
  • The back of Harald's Oslo trail map (sheet built, PZ-09 — panel not yet composited onto it), carrying the rest of the setup plus a printed stain where the ticket's glue lifted the ink. That map cannot go in an opaque sleeve — laminate both sides instead. A standalone stand-in for this panel is built: Props/Hnefatafl/build_board_setup_pdf.py.
  • Four printed journey maps, one per trail — letter size, printed and laminated at home in gloss pouches, drawn on with wet-erase marker and wiped to reset (PZ-06). No pins, cord or overlay. All four are now built by NorseBackpack/TravelMap/build_trail_maps_pdf.py. Harald's sheet (PZ-09) was the last — his two regional stops now resolve to named, sourced settlements (Syracuse; Patara, explicitly flagged as an illustrative representative point rather than an attested site). Each sheet carries a title only — the projection and coastline credit was dropped as well (Natural Earth is public domain and asks for no attribution; provenance stays in the build script and TravelMap/README.md). No trail name, no numbering, no instructions (PZ-08), and stops are unmarked so players find them by name. Answer keys build from the same script, so a key cannot drift from the sheet it answers. Label provenance and an invented-name incident are tracked in PR-10.
  • A fifth sheet: Liv’s gridded excavation site plan, in the last container with the ticket (ST-05). Direction proposed, content not decided.
  • Hand-written notes made as replaceable inserts or reusable facsimiles, with a clean master kept for reprinting.
  • A set of 18 dated postcards, one per route stop — the spine of the game, released a few at a time. Only the first card starts outside the bag.
  • The branch-rune cipher now lives on Harald's trail map itself, not on a separate stick or museum label — see PZ-02 and Props & specs.
  • A comb with broken teeth, used as a grille over postcard messages.
  • Mixed coins, a merchant’s fictional value key, and a calculator or four-wheel treasure tally. No scale is required (PR-24/25).
  • Reader tools: a magnifier, a red acetate filter, and a UV pen with a blacklight.
  • Replica object labels, family record cards, saga strips, a travel wallet and a fictional boarding pass.

Design for repeat play: no writing on irreplaceable props, all loose pieces inventoried, and every puzzle given a clear reset state.

End-of-game keepsake · revised 25 September 2026

A small adventure medallion

One solid print, no glue. A small keepsake with a shared Escape Backpack back and an adventure-specific crest. The selected concept is the right-hand shield raven front and the right-hand compass back.

Current model · v2

  • 50 mm diameter × 4.4 mm total thickness. A 3.8 mm body with 0.6 mm raised crest. Thicknesses use 0.2 mm layer increments; the 0.4 mm nozzle constrains feature width.
  • Front faces up: detailed raven with layered neck, wing and tail feathers, eye and beak, shield and four ornaments. Fine channels widened by 0.16 mm; two isolated fragments too narrow for a 0.4 mm line removed. This is flat relief, without the concept image's metallic texture or sculpted shading.
  • Back faces down: “ADVENTURE” and “COMPLETE” curve around the compass, with “ESCAPE BACKPACK” below. Text and compass are engraved 0.4 mm into the underside in the body colour. Actual font outlines are measured; text clears the engraved border by at least 1.21 mm. Underside geometry is mirrored so the finished back reads normally.
  • Optional crest colour: Prusa i3 MK3S+ / 0.4 mm nozzle; use 0.20 mm layers including the first layer, then one manual filament swap before layer 20, at Z = 4.00 mm. Body ends at 3.80 mm; crest fills the final three layers. Rim stays the body colour. One print job; no swap needed for a single colour. Prusa colour-change instructions.

Open one-piece STL · Open print-file ZIP · Print notes

PR-28 · Physical print pending. One closed connected mesh, 50 × 50 × 4.4 mm; back text placement and readability checked in CAD. No printer-profile slice or physical print yet. Check underside letter counters, first-layer spread, shallow bridges and smallest feather tips on the first print. Start without supports or raft; use the profile for the chosen filament. The STL contains geometry only; add the optional colour-change pause in the slicer.

Artwork source, exact prompt and build method

Built-in ImageGen used the front reference sheet (right coin only) to make the detailed crest mask. The back reference sheet informed the lettering and compass arrangement.

Exact prompt: Use case: precise-object-edit. Input image is the reference; use ONLY the RIGHT-HAND shield raven medallion. Convert its face artwork into a single clean orthographic black-and-white relief manufacturing mask on a white square canvas. Preserve the distinctive left-facing raven head, strong hooked beak, round eye, layered neck feathers, and all the overlapping wing and tail feather groups; preserve the peaked shield outline and the four geometric ornaments outside the shield. Reconstruct the right coin face head-on, undistorted. No text, no coin outer rim, no coin blank, no shadow, no metal texture, no grey, no gradients, no perspective, no captions. Black means solid raised detail; white means exposed base. Raven must have an elegant cohesive silhouette with internal WHITE grooves separating about 12 to 18 substantial overlapping feathers, an eye cutout, and a beak separation groove. Keep fine details bold enough to be resolved at a 40 mm artwork width with a 0.4 mm nozzle: feather-separating white gaps about 0.5 mm, major raised strokes about 0.8 to 1.2 mm. Maintain the original shield raven character rather than replacing it with a generic cartoon bird. Entire crest plus ornaments centered with generous white margin. Square image, crisp vector-like contours.

Props/Medallion/build_medallion_v2.py traces the alpha-composited mask, scales to a 21.8 mm radius before widening channels, lays out Arial Bold outlines on arcs and builds a closed mesh from polygon layers. The compass uses outline engraving to keep underside bridges short. Front SVG · Readable back SVG · Geometry measurements · Mesh checks.

Superseded · first glue-together prototype

The first export incorrectly changed the intended single print into two 2.2 mm halves to glue together, oversimplified the crest and did not fit the lettering properly. The user rejected that approach on 25 September 2026. Its files remain in Props/Medallion/Superseded_v1/ as history; use the v2 one-piece STL above.

Structure · folded in from the Props & choices tab, 17 Sept 2026

Containers and release

Not decided, on purpose. The bag was previously divided into six named containers — main compartment, archive case, RAVEN, CROWN and WELL pouches, and a travel wallet — each with a fixed code. That list and those codes have been removed. How many containers there are, what they are called and what each one opens with will be decided once the postcard puzzles exist, so the containers can be shaped around the puzzles rather than the other way round. Tracked as ST-01 to ST-04 in the Open questions tab.

Two codes survive the change. The opening four-digit code belongs to the bag itself rather than to any named container, and the final route code 1972 comes from the shapes the four trails draw. Confirm the lock hardware supports four digits before fixing either.

Reset and prototype checks

Repack from the innermost container outwards, leaving only postcard L1 and the two luggage tags outside. Scramble every lock. Keep a packing photo and a numbered spare postcard set. The exact order cannot be written until the container structure is decided (ST-01).

Before final production: make every date and place name unambiguous; confirm every route point is easy to locate; test that the completed lines resemble only the intended digits; run an unfamiliar group through the paper version. Observe solve times before choosing a duration target.

Current assumptions. mostly adults with potential teen players, no prior Norse knowledge, a table available, and reusable props throughout.

Superseded snapshot: the old “Decided” summary

Kept because it was a decision record, not deleted. Parts of it are now wrong: postmarks carry no date field at all (PC-02/PC-03/PC-04), the deck is a candidate 22 rather than 18 (PZ-18), and ordering is reconstructed from evidence rather than read off interleaved postmark dates. The current text is in the Postcard system and Final riddle tabs.

Decided

  1. The postcards are the main mechanic. Everything else in the bag is an instrument that acts on them. See the Postcard system tab.
  2. Opening puzzle: two luggage tags on the bag, two stand-out digits each; postcard L1 names L’Anse aux Meadows, and the tag showing that place reads first. Four-digit code; the digits themselves are open (PC-01).
  3. Postcards: one per route stop, so 18 cards at roughly 5 × 3.5 in.
  4. Every card is active — all 18 do a job besides the final ordering. This replaces the earlier “five or six active, the rest quiet” decision.
  5. Card design: stamp motif (trail), postmark (date and place), a printed publisher’s caption carrying real trivia, and her handwriting carrying the rules. Anomaly stamps and tilted stamps are dropped.
  6. Dates are interleaved across the four trails, so the endgame needs splitting by stamp before ordering by date.
  7. Final puzzle: four printed maps, one per trail, plus all 18 cards. Drawing each trail’s legs in order gives the four digits.
  8. Cipher: branch runes (kvistrúnir), answering HRAFN.
  9. Tools in the bag: magnifier, red acetate filter, UV pen and blacklight.
  10. Non-lock clues: three, whose answers are instructions rather than codes.
Basis and limits. Read the text in Norse_Ideas.pptx, Ideas-Designs.pptx, Hiking’s clue and reassembly documents and support workbook; inspected Aurora_Visual_Guide.html. The existing Norse deck supplies the aunt, family origins, Norway invitation, runes, board game and mythology ideas. This page proposes everything else. Original slide artwork and physical backpacks were not inspected.

Historical references: British Museum: hnefatafl; Museum of Gaming: an example rule set; National Museum of Denmark: Viking Age runes. Viking Age rune alphabets had 16 characters with some shared sounds. The aunt’s cipher, saga, genealogy and solo board rules are invented for this game.

Reference

Design guide

The visual system this page already uses, written down so new content — stamp artwork, later props, future tabs — stays consistent with it. Nothing here is a new decision; it's what the page already does, made explicit.

Core chrome first, then the two accent colours used for status and emphasis, then the four trail colours (also used for the stamp series, set out below). Illustration-specific colours inside individual SVG diagrams aren't part of this system and are chosen per diagram.

Typography

Display · Cinzel
The Raven Inheritance

Headings only (h1, h2), the route digits and the trailhead numerals. A local file, loaded from Fonts/Cinzel. Carries the "museum label" formality.

Body & UI · Segoe UI / Arial
Buttons, navigation, running text, table content, eyebrows.

The workhorse font for everything that isn't a heading, a quote, or a code.

Quotes & codes · Georgia
"I could use someone who notices what everyone else walks past."

Blockquotes, lock codes (.code), the rune-symbol display, and postcard message copy. A warmer serif for anything meant to feel hand-written or old.

Postcard system · standard

Card back: the standard layout and type scale

Set on card L1 and approved as the standard for all eighteen. Built in build_postcard_L1_pdf.py; measurements are in PDF points on a 5 × 3.5 in card (360 × 252 pt).

What changed, and why. The right column carried a 60 × 72 pt dead strip to the left of the stamp, while the address panel was 150 pt wide for a longest line of 78 pt — half of it unused. The address block moved up into that strip, the stamp now overlaps its unused top-right corner as it would on a real card, and the address rules stop at x = 278 rather than running under the stamp. That freed 44 pt of height, and the Fun Fact block grew from 48 pt to 89 pt — which is what let every size below go up.

Postcard system · visual identity

The four stamp series

Colour and motif are now built for all four trails. Aud’s original high-seat pillars and Harald’s original labrys were replaced after the history review; the current comb and single-bladed Dane axe artwork is shown below.

TrailColourMotifWhy
Leif Erikson #216580A longshipThe obvious, strongest read for a Vinland voyage; matches the route-map colour already used for this trail.
Rollo & descendants #a2562dThe Bayeux cometHalley's Comet as depicted in the Bayeux Tapestry (captioned ISTI MIRANT STELLA). Ties directly to Bayeux, a stop already on this trail, and avoids a second ship motif competing with Leif's.
Aud the Deep-Minded #6d528bA comb BuiltWas high-seat pillars (öndvegissúlur); that is Ingólfr Arnarson’s story, not hers. Landnámabók says Kambsnes is named for the comb Aud lost there — documented, specific to her, and it ties the stamp to the comb prop and to the candidate five-letter answer KAMBR. Replacement artwork built 20 Sept 2026 (HI-02).
Harald Hardrada #467444A Dane axe BuiltWas a labrys, which is Minoan, not Norse. The Varangian Guard were the emperor’s axe-bearing guard and carried the Dane axe: a single broad blade on a long two-handed haft. Still deliberately not his raven banner (Landeyðan), which would compete with the game’s own raven/HRAFN emblem. Replacement artwork built 20 Sept 2026 (HI-01).

Flat background treatment applied to all four series: the area outside the perforations is transparent, so each stamp sits as a printed-stamp overlay on the postcard stock. Aud and Harald were regenerated with corrected motifs on 20 Sept 2026. Before printing the full deck, confirm that all four remain clear at roughly 20×24 mm.

Illustration brief used for the first pass

One shared style across all four series, so they read as one issuer's stamps rather than four unrelated pictures. These briefs produced the artwork shown above and remain the reference for later revisions.

Shared style, all four: single motif, centred, filling most of the frame. Engraved-line illustration in the style of a Victorian-to-Edwardian postage stamp — fine cross-hatching for shading, no flat colour fills — printed in one dominant ink colour (the trail colour) on a lightly tinted paper ground, not full-colour. A plain perforated border, no denomination, no lettering, no country name, and no date: those are either added later per-card (price, postmark) or intentionally left out. Portrait or landscape orientation, whichever suits the motif, consistent within a series across all its cards.

The screen-size review is complete. Sanity-check that all four remain readable and distinguishable at true stamp size (roughly 20×24 mm on the finished card) before producing every card's stamp from them.

Tone & voice

Carried over from the old Props & choices tab's presentation direction, which was folded into Props & specs on 17 Sept 2026. This is the canonical copy; the duplicate there was dropped.

Playful mystery

An adventurous aunt, dry museum humour, danger kept fictional and light. Avoid horror, solemn ritual, or dense historical exposition.

Clever, not childish

Challenge comes from connecting evidence, handling objects and spotting patterns — not trivia, tiny print or obscure rune knowledge.

Mostly adults, also teens

Assume no prior Norse knowledge. Clue wording stays direct even when the props carry the atmosphere.

Difficulty lever

A notch harder than Hiking, via convergence — at least two codes needing three separate props — not obscurity.

Liv's postcard voice

Enthusiastic, warm, fun, friendly — on every one of the 18 handwritten messages, not just card L1's. Confirmed against card L1 (“Hello, nephew!” … “With love, Aunt Liv”) and card L2's decided draft (“…something with claws walked past the cabin last night — I didn’t sleep a wink!” Changed from “my tent” and rebuilt, 17 Sept 2026 (PZ-18): the itinerary puts Battle Harbour on 10 February, and the island is boat-access with no winter camping. The joke is unchanged., PZ-05). A small aside or joke reads better than a flat factual sentence. Any puzzle-mechanism nudge stays out of this voice entirely — it hides in production-metadata elements (imprints, credits), never in her handwriting.

Components

Buttons & navigation

Solid green for the primary action or the active nav tab; an outlined neutral button everywhere else. No third button style.

Tags & pills

TagPill · defaultPill · openPill · test

Tags label the whole page or a whole card (top of the header, "Decided" lists). Pills are Design-spec-tab only and carry status: green = done/default, rust = open question, brown = needs a test.

Cards & panels

The single reusable content container: cream-white fill, a thin warm-grey border, a soft corner radius, generous padding. Called .panel outside the Props & specs tab and .card inside it — same look, scoped separately so the two tabs' styles can't leak into each other.

Notes & warnings

A left-border callout in place of an icon: amber border for a note worth flagging, rust border for something that actually blocks progress. Used sparingly — a page of warnings stops reading as a warning.

Tables

Minimal ruled rows, no vertical lines, small-caps-style uppercase headers in muted grey. Used for any structured comparison (stamp series, container contents, route stops) rather than building one-off layouts.

Icons & illustration

Bayeux-style rebus and margin icons

Built: ten tiny drawings by Aunt Liv for postcard backs: cross, horizontal recurve bow, lightning bolt, flattened tree, teacup with saucer, Roman numeral I, arrow/2 hybrid, plain “to” arrow, fish-scale patch and a face-on die showing five. The set takes its irregular outline, naive proportions and restrained blue-black, terracotta, ochre and sage-blue palette from the Bayeux Tapestry reference, while remaining clean transparent artwork rather than a photographed textile. Each production PNG is 1024 × 1024 px at 300 dpi with genuine alpha. The original single-colour #A2562D five-icon set remains in the same folder as the superseded v1.

Placement: intended as Liv's own drawings on the postcard backs. Which symbol belongs on which card, and the rebus order, are still unassigned (PC-15); no card build has been changed. Files: production assets and Bayeux_Margin_Symbols_v1_PrintScale_Preview.png live in Postcards/RebusIcons/; the ImageGen source renders and a lightweight crop of the public-domain reference are retained in Postcards/RebusIcons/Sources/. The I, arrow/2 and die source renders contained a baked checkerboard despite the alpha request; the plain arrow and fish scales arrived with genuine alpha. prepare_bayeux_margin_symbols.py handles both cases and normalizes all five to 1024 px at 300 dpi.

Superseded v1 production prompt
Use case: illustration-story
Asset type: tiny transparent rebus icon for a printable postcard
Style reference: visual language of Bayeux Tapestry registered figures — simplified, almost naive medieval silhouette and compact proportions — but rendered as a clean modern flat graphic, not as a photograph of embroidery.
Style/medium: strict two-dimensional vector-flat appliqué icon. Use bold simple contour shapes and solid fill only. Suggest couched embroidery only through the sturdy silhouette and a very small number of broad graphic seams; do not draw individual threads or fibers.
Composition/framing: one isolated object, compact silhouette, centered, generous transparent margin
Color palette: every visible non-transparent pixel must be rust-brown #A2562D only. No darker outline, no lighter interior, no black, no glow, no tonal variation.
Transparency: genuine transparent alpha outside the object. No semi-transparent halo, colored aura, checkerboard, backing rectangle, or background.
Constraints: simple enough to read at roughly 0.4-0.5 inches wide on a postcard margin; no text, no border, no shadow, no linen/fabric texture, no yarn texture, no cross-hatching, no shading, no gradient, no bevel, no lighting, no watermark, no extra objects. This is a flat printed icon, not a physical embroidered patch.

Each icon appended one Primary request and Output requirement:

Cross — Primary request: A plain upright Latin cross, no ornamentation.
Output requirement: one isolated cross only. Favor large solid rust shapes and clear negative space over detail.

Bow — Primary request: A simple recurve hunting bow held horizontally, strung, no arrow nocked.
Output requirement: one isolated bow only. Favor large solid rust shapes and clear negative space over detail.

Lightning bolt — Primary request: A single jagged lightning-bolt shape, angular zigzag, no cloud, no rain.
Output requirement: one isolated lightning bolt only. Favor large solid rust shapes and clear negative space over detail.

Tree — Primary request: A single stylised tree with a rounded canopy on a plain trunk, in the flattened tree style used in the Bayeux Tapestry's border scenes, no ground line.
Output requirement: one isolated tree only. Favor large solid rust shapes and clear negative space over detail.

Teacup — Primary request: A simple handled teacup on a small saucer, plain, no steam, no pattern.
Output requirement: one isolated teacup only. Favor large solid rust shapes and clear negative space over detail.

Museum ticket front · PZ-12

Built: 2 × 5.5 in portrait artwork at 300 dpi. The fictional museum header occupies the top inch; the rest is a clean-map-style visitor site plan with coastline, paths, buildings, ticket furniture and nine equal-weight numbered scenery markers. The marker locations now agree with the numbered room groups on the back: entrance/visitor centre 1, excavation 2, quay/boat work 3, crafts 4, forge 5, longhouse 6, Jarl’s quarters 7, great hall/ocean gallery 8, and an outlying palisaded building 9. They remain ordinary wayfinding scenery with no route or hierarchy.

Files: print asset Props/MuseumTicket/Museum_Ticket_Front_300dpi_v2.png (600 × 1650 px, 300 dpi); original ImageGen render retained as Museum_Ticket_Front_Source_v2.png.

Back built: the A–Z visitor index from PZ-12, laid out as one alphabetical column (not two — a 2 in-wide ticket doesn’t fit two legible columns once the real front art pinned the physical size), zebra-striped for scanning, in the same header/rule/palette treatment as the front. Built by Props/MuseumTicket/build_museum_ticket_pdf.py, which combines the front image and the drawn back into one print-ready card and a letter-sheet version with crop marks. Output: output/pdf/Museum_Ticket_Print.pdf, Museum_Ticket_Letter_Print.pdf.

Exact ImageGen prompt
Use case: infographic-diagram
Asset type: print-ready front artwork for a fictional museum admission ticket
Primary request: Create a complete portrait ticket design for the fictional “Viking Museum of L’Anse aux Meadows.”
Input image: style reference only. Match the first row labelled “Clean map”: strong clean geometric line art, rounded joins, flat limited colors, no painterly texture.
Canvas: extremely tall narrow 4:11 aspect ratio, corresponding to 2 × 5.5 inches. Full bleed. Keep all important content safely inside the edges.
Composition: the top roughly 18% is a strong header band. The remaining area is a simplified visitor site map viewed from above, filling the ticket. Include a coastline/water edge, branching footpaths, several simple building footprints, a tiny dock, parking area, and sparse low shrubs. The site plan should feel plausible but is fictional and schematic.
Header text (verbatim, prominent, spelled exactly): “VIKING MUSEUM” on the first line and “OF L’ANSE AUX MEADOWS” on the second line.
Ticket text (verbatim): “GENERAL ADMISSION”
Stub text (verbatim, small): “No. 0847”
Map markers: exactly nine circular numbered markers, showing each integer 1 through 9 once. Place them naturally across the site map. All nine markers must have identical size, color, visual weight, and treatment. Do not connect them, arrange them into a path, align them into a shape, or emphasize any marker.
Optional furniture: one very small simple compass rose marked only with N.
Style/medium: polished vector-like schematic visitor map, crisp flat line art, screen-print clarity, suitable for physical prop printing.
Color palette: forest green #283B34 for main ink, muted rust #B56A2A for accent, warm light cream background. Water may be a very pale desaturated green-blue while remaining within the restrained palette.
Typography: sturdy museum-signage typography, highly legible, no faux runes.
Constraints: exact spelling and punctuation; exactly nine numbered markers; fictional museum; clean map only; no legend tying numbers to names; no A–Z index; no meaningful number pattern; no QR code; no barcode; no logos; no watermark.
Avoid: painted illustration, photorealism, antique parchment distress, ornamental knotwork, Viking clichés, perspective buildings, extra digits, duplicated digits, route arrows, dotted routes, highlighted destination, visual puzzle, or any suggestion that the numbered markers encode a message.

Version 2 revision prompt
Use case: precise-object-edit
Asset type: revised front artwork for the fictional Viking Museum of L’Anse aux Meadows admission ticket
Input image: edit target. Preserve the complete ticket’s tall 4:11 composition, header, typography, clean-map line-art style, cream ground, forest-green #283B34 ink, muted-rust #B56A2A accents, coastline, and uncluttered visitor-map character.

Primary request: Revise the numbered site features so the map visually agrees with the museum’s room-number index, while keeping the numbers as ordinary museum wayfinding markers rather than a visible puzzle.

Make these targeted content changes:
1 — place beside the entrance/visitor-centre building nearest the parking and main path.
2 — place beside a small archaeological excavation area containing an open dig outline, one standing rune stone, and rough ground.
3 — place beside the dock/quay and boat-work area, with a small boat shed or keel frame nearby.
4 — place beside a modest craft area: sod-house form plus a few small dye-vat/workshop details.
5 — place beside a compact forge/hearth/tool-shed building, identifiable by one small chimney.
6 — place beside a clear longhouse: long rectangular building with a pitched roof.
7 — place beside a separate dignified but restrained Jarl’s quarters building.
8 — place beside the largest hall building near the coast, suitable for a Great Hall/Ocean Gallery.
9 — place off to one side in the upper meadow beside a small outlying building enclosed by a simple wooden palisade or fenced compound. This must not be the dock.

Text changes:
- Preserve “VIKING MUSEUM”, “OF L’ANSE AUX MEADOWS”, and “GENERAL ADMISSION” exactly.
- Replace the small stub number with the exact text “No. 084726”.

Invariants:
- Exactly nine circular markers, showing each integer 1 through 9 once.
- All markers remain identical in size, rust color, typography, and visual weight.
- No legend, room names, A–Z index, arrows, connecting route, ordering cues, emphasized marker, or hidden number shape.
- The locations may be scattered; do not arrange 1–9 as a readable sequence.
- Preserve full bleed and safe margins.
- Keep a small north compass.
- Keep the design flat, crisp, schematic, and consistent with the original; no painted texture, photorealism, faux runes, knotwork, QR code, barcode, logo, or watermark.
- Change only the map feature assignments and stub number described above; retain the established ticket identity and palette.
ᚱ
No generic icon set. The page uses real Younger Futhark runes as decoration (the header's giant faint watermark, the rune-choice buttons) rather than a symbol library. It reinforces that the cipher is a real writing system, not a game icon.
●
Swatch dots (.dot/.swatch) are the only abstract icon in use, for legends: small, flat, colour-coded squares identifying a trail or a design channel at a glance — as seen in the postcard corpus worksheet and the colour swatches on this page.
◆
Hand-drawn line diagrams (the postcard-back map, the hnefatafl board, the branch-rune figures) share one muted parchment palette — #f0e6cf fill, #a99a7b/#8a7d63 stroke — instead of being illustrated individually. New diagrams should reuse those two stroke tones rather than introducing new ones.

Alpha correction
ImageGen returned a grey-and-white checkerboard baked into RGB twice rather than transparent alpha. Art/Raven/remove_checkerboard_background.js removes only the repeated neutral checker colours, including the enclosed patch between the legs, while preserving the varied pale engraving strokes. The checked result is Raven_Profile_Icon_v1.png.

Built, not yet placed: six additional 1024 px transparent icons for the paired English-word concepts Combat/Fight, Beef/Cow, Forest/Woodland, Poultry/Hen, People/Folk and Tavern/Inn. Each pair names one concept, so there are six icons rather than twelve. The production assets use the same exact #A2562D ink and cleanup as the existing Rollo vignettes. Forest, folk and inn needed a second ImageGen background-extraction pass because their first renders contained a baked checkerboard; only the corrected transparent renders are retained as sources.

Exact ImageGen prompts 
Shared block (all six initial generations)
Use case: style-transfer
Asset type: tiny transparent decorative vignette for printable Map 2 of a Viking treasure map
Input images: style references only; match their flat rust-brown antique screen-print treatment, line weight, lightly weathered edges, and compact readable silhouette
Style/medium: very simple flat hand-pulled screen-print map icon; elegant antique adventure-map ornament; minimal internal detail
Composition/framing: one isolated centered icon, compact silhouette, generous transparent margin
Color palette: rust-brown #A2562D only
Constraints: genuinely transparent background with alpha; simple enough to read at about 0.4 inches wide; thin-to-medium outline; no text, no border, no frame, no shadow, no paper texture, no gradient, no cross-hatching, no watermark, no unrelated objects.

Combat / Fight · Primary request
A crossed medieval sword and single-bladed battle axe, forming a clean balanced X. Immediately recognizable as combat or fighting; no shield, no blood, no severed parts.

Beef / Cow · Primary request
A sturdy domestic cow standing in clean side profile, facing right, immediately recognizable by bovine muzzle, ears, small horns, udder, hooves, and tail. Natural proportions; not a bull, ox, bison, or calf.

Forest / Woodland · Primary request
A compact woodland cluster of three distinct mature deciduous trees with overlapping leafy crowns and visible trunks, grounded by one restrained uneven baseline. Immediately recognizable as a forest rather than a single tree; no animals or buildings.

Poultry / Hen · Primary request
A domestic hen standing in clean side profile, facing right, immediately recognizable by small comb, short beak, rounded body, layered wing, tail feathers, and two visible legs. No eggs, nest, rooster, or chicks.

People / Folk · Primary request
A compact group of three ordinary medieval townspeople standing shoulder to shoulder, shown from about the waist up, varied simple silhouettes, immediately recognizable as people or folk. Neutral poses; no ruler, weapons, helmets, heraldry, or crowd scene.

Tavern / Inn · Primary request
A simple old timber inn represented by a compact front-facing gabled tavern facade with a central door and one small hanging tankard sign projecting from the side. Immediately recognizable as a tavern or inn; no readable text, no people, no separate objects.

Background correction used separately for Forest, Folk and Inn
Use case: background-extraction
Asset type: tiny transparent decorative vignette for printable Map 2
Primary request: Remove the entire grey-and-white checkerboard and all wrinkled background residue. Preserve [the three-tree woodland icon / the three-person folk icon / the timber inn facade with hanging tankard sign] exactly in subject, proportions, composition, rust-brown ink, weathering, and line detail.
Constraints: output must have a genuinely transparent alpha background; keep only the rust-brown icon; no checkerboard pixels, no white or grey background, no shadow, no paper texture, no new objects, no text, no border, no frame.

Château de Robert le Diable line icon · Map 2

Built, not yet placed: a sparse black architectural outline derived from W. Mechelke’s 2014 photograph of the Château de Robert le Diable. It keeps the left round tower, broken central wall, receding curtain wall, major openings and cropped right tower while omitting foliage, ground and stone texture. Production output is a 1536 × 1024 transparent PNG at 300 dpi; every visible RGB pixel is exact black. prepare_chateau_line_icon.py makes the cleanup reproducible.

Source and licence: photograph © W. Mechelke, Wikimedia Commons, CC BY-SA 3.0. Adapted into simplified line art; attribution and ShareAlike apply.
Exact ImageGen prompts 
Initial line-art generation
Use case: style-transfer
Asset type: simple architectural line icon for a printable historical map
Input image: sole structural reference; depict the Château de Robert le Diable from this exact photograph and preserve its recognizable oblique perspective and major ruin geometry
Primary request: Convert the photographed castle ruins into a sparse black line icon. Keep the left round crenellated tower, the broken central curtain wall and window openings, the receding wall, and the large cropped round tower on the right. Draw only the main silhouette and essential architectural lines.
Style/medium: clean hand-drawn black ink outline; simple confident strokes; minimal internal detail; no tonal rendering
Composition/framing: wide landscape icon matching the photo's left-to-right castle profile; centered with generous transparent margin
Color palette: solid black only
Constraints: genuinely transparent background with alpha; no white background; no grey; no colour; no filled black masses; no stone-by-stone texture; no shading; no hatching; no gradients; no sky, trees, foliage, grass, railings, path, people, text, border, shadow, watermark, or invented architecture. The castle must remain recognizable from the reference while being simple enough for small print.

Background correction
Use case: background-extraction
Asset type: black architectural line icon
Primary request: Remove the entire grey-and-white checkerboard and every wrinkled background artifact. Preserve the Château de Robert le Diable black line drawing exactly: same castle geometry, perspective, crop, stroke positions and window openings.
Color palette: solid black lines only
Constraints: output must have a genuinely transparent alpha background; keep only the black architectural lines; no white, grey or checkerboard pixels; no filled wall areas; no shading, texture, hatching, shadow, new detail, text, border, or frame.
Aud map styling pass — 18 Sept 2026. Wood, marsh and moor now use subtle non-countable textures: sparse tree marks, reeds/ripples and short moor strokes. Ordinary landmarks recede in lavender #806F96; countable crossings lead in dark plum #523A70 (bridges/gates) or dark teal #216866 (fords). The bridge is now two heavy abutment strokes, readable when rotated with a north-south road. The same treatment is implemented in TravelMap/Aud_Map_Designer.html and TravelMap/aud_layer.py; both printed map PDFs were rebuilt and visually checked.
Superseded by the selected Saga family — 19 Sept 2026. After comparing Current/A/B for all 25 legend entries, Option B was chosen for the whole map: twin wheel-track roads, walking-tick paths, double-bank rivers, wave streams, broken double ditches, leaf hedges, linked-stone walls, timber bridges, ripple fords, open gates, organic terrain marks and the filled/fragmented landmark set. Marsh is deliberately not lake-blue: it uses a muted sage-grey fill #D9DDD0 with grey-green marks #758676, while lakes retain pale blue-teal #DCE7E4. The print and answer PDFs and the editable designer use the same family.
Decided, 19 Sept 2026 — Survey Option A is the base style. The production source and editable designer now use A across the same 25 entries: outlined roads, regular dashed paths, pale-banked rivers, continuous streams, hachured ditches, even hedges, divided walls, survey crossings, sparse terrain marks and the cleaner outlined landmark family. The muted sage-grey marsh is retained so it stays distinct from the pale blue lake. Base labels now treat the feature layer as an obstacle and try right, left, above and below their marker; for example, Búðardalur moves left of its dot instead of printing through the road. The earlier Saga note remains above as the comparison history, not the current source state.
Superseded, 19 Sept 2026 — hybrid bridge-hiding trial, kept for history. Option A remains the base, with four requested exceptions: landing uses the original anchor, while gate, ford and stone wall use Option B. Bridge symbols and the Bridge legend row are hidden because road crossings over rivers already read clearly; the bridge feature data remains in place so the corrected route count of 521 is unchanged. Marsh remains muted sage-grey, and the label-avoidance placement remains active.
Refined hybrid trial — 19 Sept 2026. Roads now share a two-pass outline/fill treatment so intersecting road features merge cleanly at forks instead of one outline crossing another. Gates and all other countable crossings render above surrounding map elements; stone-wall blocks stop outside each gate’s two posts, leaving a true opening while preserving the road beneath. Ford returns to the original bank-ticks-and-three-stones mark. Bridges return with a new route-aligned design: a short, gently bowed deck with three light cross-planks, rotated to follow the nearest road, track or footpath. This supersedes the bridge-removal choice in the note above while preserving the same crossing data and route code 521. This is Aud’s final map styling, decided 19 Sept 2026 — output/pdf/Trail_Map_3_Aud_Print.pdf and _ANSWER.pdf were rebuilt under their canonical names from this exact source state. The Option A and hybrid trial PDFs, plus one further OPTION_A/HYBRID/REFINED iteration set, are archived in NorseBackpack/Drafts/2026-09-19_Aud_map_style_options/, not deleted.
